{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{58.55136pt}{-3.40334pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(3.82)

Note that the momentum-dependent structure constants
F^{abc}
and
\bar{F}^{\bar{a}\bar{b}\bar{c}}
obey the Jacobi identities of the infinite-dimensional Lie algebras
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)
and
\mathfrak{u}_{\bar{\star}}(\bar{N})
, respectively, which from this perspective can therefore also be regarded as kinematic Lie algebras. Looking at the twisted homotopy factorization (
3.39
), this means that the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory can be regarded as a double copy between the noncommutative biadjoint scalar theory and itself, with the replacement of colour by kinematics involving twisted tensor products.

Colour ordering and decomposition.

The tree-level amplitudes of the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory admit a colour decomposition on both copies of the colour algebra.
By writing the structure constants of
\mathfrak{u}(N)
as
f^{abc}=2\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}([T^{a},T^{b}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\,T^{c})
and
\mathrm{i}\,d^{abc}=2\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(\{T^{a},T^{b}\}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\,T^{c})
, the structure constants of
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)
given in
eq.

3.17
can be written as

\begin{split}F^{abc}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})&=2\,\big(\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(T^{a}\,T^{b}\,T^{c})\ \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(T^{b}\,T^{a}\,T^{c})\ \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,p_{2}\cdot\theta\,p_{1}}\big)\\
&\hskip 256.0748pt\times\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})\ .\end{split}

(3.83)

This is an explicit factorisation for a given colour ordering, a common feature of noncommutative field theories with Moyal–Weyl deformation. In the commutative limit
t\to 0
, this returns
f^{abc}
as expected.

In the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory, the same factorisation appears as a decomposition in terms of two sets of orderings of colour indices as

\begin{split}&F^{abc}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\,\bar{F}^{\bar{a}\bar{b}\bar{c}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=4\,\big(\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(T^{a}\,T^{b}\,T^{c})\ \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(T^{b}\,T^{a}\,T^{c})\ \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,p_{2}\cdot\theta\,p_{1}}\big)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\big(\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(\bar{N})}(\bar{T}^{\bar{a}}\,\bar{T}^{\bar{b}}\,\bar{T}^{\bar{c}})\ \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\bar{t}}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\bar{\theta}\,p_{2}}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(\bar{N})}(\bar{T}^{\bar{b}}\,\bar{T}^{\bar{a}}\,\bar{T}^{\bar{c}})\ \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\bar{t}}{2}\,p_{2}\cdot\bar{\theta}\,p_{1}}\big)\\
&\hskip 312.9803pt\times\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})\ .\end{split}

(3.84)

The commutative limit
t,\bar{t}\rightarrow 0
similarly reduces to
f^{abc}\,\bar{f}^{\bar{a}\bar{b}\bar{c}}
.
The rank one theory studied in
section

3.6
also follows from this formula, which for
N=\bar{N}=1
recovers only the sinus part of
eq.

3.84
.

The binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory studied here differs from the
commutative
biadjoint scalar theory of §
2.2
only in the three-point vertex. Crucially, this vertex admits a colour factorisation with only phase factors that depend solely on the orderings of momenta.
We conclude that the
n
-point partial amplitudes of the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory, defined as the summands in the expansion of the full amplitudes

\mathscr{A}^{\star\bar{\star}\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}(p,a,\bar{a})=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in S_{n}/\mathbbm{Z}_{n}\rtimes\mathbbm{Z}_{2}}\,C(\sigma)\,\bar{C}(\sigma^{\prime})\ \mathcal{A}^{\star\bar{\star}\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}\big(\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(n)\big|\sigma^{\prime}(1),\dots,\sigma^{\prime}(n)\big)\ ,

(3.85)

factor through these phases.

For example, the first term in the three-point partial amplitude given by the diagram (
3.82
) and the decomposition (
3.84
) is

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\star\bar{\star}\textrm{\tiny BAS}}(1,2,3|1,2,3)&=-4\,\mathrm{i}\,g\ \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\bar{t}}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\bar{\theta}\,p_{2}}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})\\[4.0pt]
&=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\bar{t}}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\bar{\theta}\,p_{2}}\ \mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{3}(1,2,3|1,2,3)\ ,\end{split}

(3.86)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}
is the commutative
n
-point biadjoint scalar partial amplitude, defined in
section

2.2
.

We interpret this factorisation as a slight generalisation of the well-known fact that noncommutative scalar field theory differs from its commutative counterpart only by a phase in planar graphs
[
47
,
17
]
. The upshot is that one can dress commutative colour-stripped biadjoint scalar amplitudes by the corresponding phase factors to get tree-level binoncommutative biadjoint scalar amplitudes.
This fact will be of use in §
5
.

3.8
Applications: Special galileons and self-dual gravity

As some concrete applications of the formalism we have developed thus far, as well as a glimpse towards some of our later double copy constructions, let us now look at two special “topological” realisations of the generic double copy map from the adjoint scalar theory of
section

3.4
to the theory of
section

3.6
. Here we work mostly at the level of equations of motion for brevity, but analogous statements also hold off-shell at the level of Maurer–Cartan functionals.

Zakharov–Mikhailov theory and special galileons.

In
d=2
dimensions with the Moyal–Weyl star-product (
3.1
), the adjoint scalar theory was considered in
[
28
]
as a ‘stringy deformation’ of Zakharov–Mikhailov theory
[
48
]
, which is classically equivalent to the principal chiral model. We set
t=\bar{t}=\alpha^{\prime}
, regarded as a string tension parametrizing an infinite tower of higher dimension operators, and take
\theta^{\mu\nu}=\bar{\theta}^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu}
to be the Levi–Civita symbol in two dimensions with
\epsilon^{01}=1
. Let
g=\frac{\bar{g}}{\alpha^{\prime}}
, and take the semi-classical limit
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
with
\bar{g}
finite.

Recall from
section

3.1
that in the semi-classical limit the star-commutator
\mathrm{i}\,g\,[\phi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\phi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}
is replaced by the Poisson bracket
\bar{g}\,\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\}
, where

\displaystyle\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\phi_{1}\,\partial_{\nu}\phi_{2}\ .

(3.87)

The kinematic algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
reduces to the Poisson–Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sdiff}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,1})
of area-preserving diffeomorphisms of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
, and the kinematical numerators
n_{s}
become
-4\,g\,(p_{1}\times p_{2})\,(p_{3}\times p_{4})
, and so on, where
p_{i}\times p_{j}:=\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,p_{i}{}_{\mu}\,p_{j}{}_{\nu}
.

The equations of motion (
3.43
) of the adjoint scalar theory reduce to

\displaystyle\square\,\phi-\tfrac{\bar{g}}{2}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,[\partial_{\mu}\phi,\partial_{\nu}\phi]_{\mathfrak{g}}=0\ .

(3.88)

These are just the equations of motion for the non-linear sigma-model in two dimensions, with fields
h\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,1},\mathsf{G})
, which read as
\partial^{\mu}j_{\mu}=0
for the left-invariant currents
j=h^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}h\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,1},\mathfrak{g})
. These imply that there exists an adjoint scalar
\phi\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,1},\mathfrak{g})
such that
j_{\mu}=\bar{g}\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\,\partial^{\nu}\phi
, and the Maurer–Cartan equation
\mathrm{d}j+\frac{1}{2}\,[j,j]_{\mathfrak{g}}=0
coincides with the equations of motion (
3.88
). Thus in this limit the adjoint scalar field theory for
d=2
reduces to the usual Zakharov–Mikhailov theory
[
48
]
.

On the other hand, the equation of motion (
3.72
) of the homotopy double copy theory in the limit
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
becomes

\displaystyle\square\,\phi-\tfrac{\bar{\kappa}}{4}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\{\partial_{\mu}\phi,\partial_{\nu}\phi\}=0\ ,

(3.89)

which is the equation of motion for the special galileon theory in two dimensions
[
49
,
50
,
51
]
.
The special galileon theory is invariant under the Galilean-type transformations
\phi(x)\longmapsto\phi(x)+b\cdot x+c
of the scalar field
\phi\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,1})
for
b\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
and
c\in\mathbbm{R}
, and it is quasi-isomorphic to a two-dimensional free theory.
Hence the double copy of the adjoint scalar theory can be regarded as a ‘stringy deformation’ of the special galileon theory in two dimensions.

One application of the semi-classical double copy construction is to integrability. Since the Zakharov–Mikhailov theory is classically equivalent to the principal chiral model, it is likewise integrable. Applying the colour-kinematics duality map, the special galileon theory is also integrable; see
[
28
]
for the explicit map of the Lax connection which furnishes an infinite tower of conserved currents. However, integrability does not seem to persist generally beyond the semi-classical limits in the full noncommutative theories, as we discuss below.

Self-dual Yang–Mills theory and gravity.

Self-dual Yang–Mills theory and gravity provide a four-dimensional realization of our homotopy double copy construction: for
d=2
the adjoint scalar theory was considered in
[
52
]
as a noncommutative deformation of self-dual Yang–Mills theory, while
eq.

3.72
is the equation of motion for doubly deformed self-dual gravity considered in
[
52
]
. Below we discuss this realisation in a bit more detail. In the semi-classical limit, the explicit relationship of these
d=4
theories and their double copy duality to the
d=2
theories discussed above is elucidated by
[
29
]
.

Let
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{g})
be a gauge field with curvature
F=\mathrm{d}A+\frac{\bar{g}}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}
. We decompose four-dimensional Minkowski space as
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}\simeq\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}\times\mathbbm{R}^{2}
, with light-cone coordinates
(x^{+},x^{-})
on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
and complex coordinates
(z,\bar{z})
on
\mathbbm{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbbm{C}
. Let
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
be the Hodge duality operator on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
, which acts on two-forms with
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}=-\mathbbm{1}
. Then the component form of the self-duality equation
F=\mathrm{i}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F
can be written as

\displaystyle F_{-z}=F_{+\bar{z}}=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad F_{-+}=F_{z\bar{z}}\ .

(3.90)

From
F_{-z}=0
we may choose the light-cone gauge
A_{-}=A_{z}=0
. From
F_{-+}=F_{z\bar{z}}
it follows that there exists an adjoint scalar
\phi\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{g})
such that
A_{+}=-\partial_{z}\phi
and
A_{\bar{z}}=-\partial_{-}\phi
; the scalar field
\phi
represents the single polarization state remaining in a gluon after projection to the self-dual sector. The remaining equation
F_{+\bar{z}}=0
then yields

\displaystyle\square\,\phi-\bar{g}\,[\partial_{z}\phi,\partial_{-}\phi]_{\mathfrak{g}}=0\ ,

(3.91)

where
\square=-\partial_{+}\,\partial_{-}+\partial_{z}\,\partial_{\bar{z}}
, which is just the equation of motion (
3.88
) with
\mu,\nu\in\{z,-\}
. In this case the semi-classical kinematic algebra generates area-preserving diffeomorphisms of the null
(z,x^{-})
-plane, which is the known kinematic Lie algebra of self-dual Yang–Mills theory
[
6
]
. The corresponding semi-classical limit of the Maurer–Cartan functional (
3.44
) for the adjoint scalar theory is the cubic action functional for self-dual Yang–Mills theory in the Leznov gauge
[
53
,
54
,
55
]
.

The analogue in asymptotically flat gravity is the self-duality equation for the Riemann curvature tensor, which encodes both the equations of motion and the algebraic Bianchi identity. With the light-cone gauge choice for the metric, the semi-classical double copy equation of motion (
3.89
) coincides with Plebański’s second heavenly equation for self-dual gravity
[
56
]
:

\displaystyle\square\,\phi-\tfrac{\bar{\kappa}}{2}\,\{\partial_{z}\phi,\partial_{-}\phi\}=0\ ,

(3.92)

where here the scalar field
\phi\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
represents the positive helicity state of the graviton, and the constant
\bar{\kappa}
controls the deformation away from flat space. This involves two copies of the area-preserving diffeomorphism algebra. The corresponding semi-classical limit of the Maurer–Cartan functional (
3.73
) is the action functional for the string field theory of
\mathcal{N}=2
strings
[
57
]
.

Like the Zakharov–Mikhailov and special galileon theories, self-dual Yang–Mills theory and gravity are integrable theories, each admitting an infinite tower of conserved charges: On the gauge theory side there is a Lax pair
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M})
, which with our gauge choice is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\partial_{+}-\lambda\,(\partial_{\bar{z}}-\partial_{+}\phi)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{M}=\partial_{z}-\lambda\,(\partial_{-}-\partial_{z}\phi)

(3.93)

for a spectral parameter
\lambda\in\mathbbm{C}\mathbbm{P}^{1}
, and analogously on the gravity side where integrability is linked to its infinite-dimensional
w_{1+\infty}
symmetry.
This is consistent with Ward’s conjecture: all integrable theories are related to self-dual Yang–Mills theory by replacing its structure constants
f^{abc}
by other structure constants, which can be thought of as a “symmetry reduction”. Here we observe the natural sequence of symmetry reductions similarly to
eq.

1.9
, starting from the biadjoint scalar theory of
section

2.2
, to self-dual Yang–Mills theory, and finally to self-dual gravity.

Let us now look at the generic case of finite deformation parameters
t\neq\bar{t}
, with the understanding as above that spacetime indices are always restricted to directions along the null
(z,x^{-})
-plane. Then the Maurer–Cartan equation (
3.72
) for the homotopy double copy theory coincides with the “doubly-deformed” Plebański equation for self-dual gravity considered in
[
52
]
. This theory of noncommutative gravity is not integrable, due to the breakdown of the Jacobi identities. In the semi-classical limit
\bar{t}\to 0
it reduces to the deformed Plebański equation of noncommutative gravity
[
58
,
59
,
60
]

\displaystyle\square\,\phi-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\bar{\kappa}}{2}\,[\partial_{z}\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\partial_{-}\phi]_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}=0\ .

(3.94)

This is now integrable with an infinite tower of conserved currents; integrability here relies crucially on the semi-classical form of the kinematic structure constants
\bar{F}_{0}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
of the symplecton
W_{1+\infty}
algebra
[
52
]
. The Moyal–Weyl deformation of self-dual gravity is one-loop exact and has also appeared in recent parallel discussions of celestial holography
[
61
,
62
,
63
,
45
,
64
]
; in
[
65
]
it is interpreted as the
T\overline{T}
-deformation of self-dual gravity.

Consider now what happens beyond the semi-classical limit of the adjoint scalar theory. The self-dual Yang–Mills equations are then deformed to

\displaystyle\square\,\phi+\tfrac{g}{2}\,\llbracket\phi,\phi\rrbracket_{\textrm{\tiny AS}}=0\ ,

(3.95)

where the bracket
\llbracket-,-\rrbracket_{\textrm{\tiny AS}}
is defined in
section

3.4
.
This is obtained as one of two single copies of deformed self-dual gravity in the semi-classical limit, as
\bar{t}\to 0
alone, of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
3.69
): replacing the inner kinematical vector space
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}
with the colour algebra
\mathfrak{g}
gives the standard self-dual Yang–Mills theory above, which is integrable, whereas replacing the outer factor of
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}
with
\mathfrak{g}
leads to the theory with deformed equations of motion (
3.95
), which is not integrable. The main technical issue is that the bracket operation
\llbracket-,-\rrbracket_{\textrm{\tiny AS}}
is not a Lie bracket, as it violates the (ungraded) Jacobi identities.

As we have shown in
section

3.5
,
eq.

3.95
are the natural gauge field equations that arise in the homotopy double copy prescription and which are consistent with colour-kinematics duality. However they differ from the field equations of the usual noncommutative self-dual Yang–Mills theory, which has a non-trivial commutative limit to ordinary self-dual Yang–Mills theory and is defined using the star-commutator bracket:

\displaystyle\square\,\phi-{\mathrm{i}\,g}\,[\partial_{z}\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\partial_{-}\phi]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=0\ .

(3.96)

These are the Euler–Lagrange equations which follow from varying the noncommutative cubic action functional
[
66
]

\displaystyle S_{\text{\tiny YM+}}^{\star}[\phi]=\int\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\ \mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,\phi\star\square\,\phi-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,g}{3!}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\phi\star[\partial_{\mu}\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\partial_{\nu}\phi]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big)

(3.97)

for the Leznov prepotential
\phi\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
.

Noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(N)
self-dual Yang–Mills theory with action functional (
3.97
) is the semi-classical limit
\bar{t}\to 0
of the rank
\bar{N}=1
binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory of
section

3.7
in
d=4
dimensions.
As we discussed in
section

3.1
, the star-commutator bracket is not compatible with standard colour-kinematics duality, due to the appearance of anticommutators of Lie algebra generators in
eq.

3.12
which obstructs an immediate (untwisted) homotopy factorisation of the
L_{\infty}
-structure. On the other hand, this theory is integrable
[
67
,
68
]
.

The noncommutative instanton equation (
3.96
) in the rank one limit
N=1
coincides with the noncommutative Plebański equation (
3.94
), while the action functional (
3.97
) for
N=1
is the semi-classical limit of the Maurer–Cartan functional (
3.73
) as
\bar{t}\to 0
.
It follows that self-dual
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
Yang–Mills theory is the double copy of the adjoint scalar theory with ordinary self-dual Yang–Mills theory, for any gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. This double copy theory is precisely noncommutative self-dual gravity. In particular, the integrability of gravity is “inherited” from the corresponding gauge theories via the double copy and is guaranteed by integrability of at least one of the two gauge theory single copies
[
52
,
61
]
.
In what follows we shall assert that such double copy interpretations of rank one noncommutative gauge theories hold in general.

4
Noncommutative Chern–Simons theory

4.1
Moyal–Weyl deformation of differential forms

To study general noncommutative gauge theories, we first discuss the extension of the Moyal–Weyl deformation of scalar fields from §
3.1
to include differential forms of arbitrary degree. Let
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
be the exterior algebra of differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
valued in the matrix Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{u}(N)
. This is a strict
A_{\infty}
-algebra with the de Rham differential
\mathrm{d}
and the composition of matrix multiplication with the exterior product of forms, which as usual we make into a strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra under the commutator bracket:

\displaystyle[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=\alpha\wedge\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\beta\wedge\alpha\ ,

(4.1)

where
|\alpha|
denotes the degree of a homogeneous form
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
.

As a vector space

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))\simeq\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\ ,

(4.2)

and this is also true at the level of homotopy algebras: the strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
factorizes into the tensor product of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(N)
with the strict
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\alpha&=\mathrm{d}(T^{a}\otimes\alpha_{a})=T^{a}\otimes\mathrm{d}\alpha_{a}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}&=[T^{a}\otimes\alpha_{a},T^{b}\otimes\beta_{b}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=[T^{a},T^{b}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\otimes(\alpha_{a}\wedge\beta_{b})\ ,\end{split}

(4.3)

where
\alpha_{a},\beta_{b}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
and we used graded commutativity of the exterior product of forms:
\alpha_{a}\wedge\beta_{b}=(-1)^{|\alpha_{a}|\,|\beta_{b}|}\,\beta_{b}\wedge\alpha_{a}
. This is a special instance of a more general statement that is relevant for us: The tensor product of any
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}
with
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

The Moyal–Weyl deformation of
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is defined by deforming the exterior product to the star-product

\displaystyle\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta:=\alpha\wedge\,\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\ \overset{\leftarrow}{\pounds}_{\mu}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\overset{\rightarrow}{\pounds}_{\nu}\Big)\,\beta\ ,

(4.4)

where
\pounds_{\mu}:=\pounds_{\partial_{\mu}}
are Lie derivatives along the holonomic frame of vector fields on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
. The conventional noncommutative gauge theories are realised by deforming the commutator (
4.1
) to the star-commutator on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
:

\displaystyle[\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\beta]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\beta\wedge_{\star}\alpha\ .

(4.5)

This bracket again makes
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
into a strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

As before, while the vector space isomorphism (
4.2
) still holds, it is no longer an isomorphism of strict
L_{\infty}
-algebras, as

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\beta]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=[T^{a}\otimes\alpha_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}T^{b}\otimes\beta_{b}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}&=\tfrac{1}{2}\,[T^{a},T^{b}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\otimes\{\alpha_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\beta_{b}\}_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}\\
&\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\{T^{a},T^{b}\}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\otimes[\alpha_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\beta_{b}]_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}\ ,\end{split}

(4.6)

where
\{\alpha_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\beta_{b}\}_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}:=\alpha_{a}\wedge_{\star}\beta_{b}+(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\beta_{b}\wedge_{\star}\alpha_{a}
. As previously, in what follows we shall understand colour-stripping in noncommutative gauge theories in a twisted sense, which is compatible with colour-kinematics duality in this setting.

4.2
The
L_{\infty}
-structure of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory

Consider standard noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(N)
Chern–Simons gauge theory on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
, which is defined by the action functional

\displaystyle S_{\textrm{\tiny CS}}^{\star}[A]=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A+\frac{g}{3!}\,A\wedge_{\star}[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big)\ ,

(4.7)

where
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is the gauge field and
g=\sqrt{2\pi/k}
is the gauge coupling constant with
k
the Chern–Simons level.
Solutions of the corresponding field equation are flat noncommutative connections,
F_{A}^{\star}=0
, where

\displaystyle F_{A}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{g}{2}\,[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}

(4.8)

is the field strength in
\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
.
The noncommutative Chern–Simons functional is invariant under infinitesimal star-gauge transformations
\delta^{\star}_{c}A=\mathrm{d}c+g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}
with
c\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
.
The Bianchi identity
\nabla_{A}^{\star}F^{\star}_{A}=0
in
\Omega^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is the Noether identity corresponding to this gauge symmetry, where
\nabla_{A}^{\star}:\Omega^{p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is the covariant derivative

\displaystyle\nabla_{A}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}+g\,[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}-]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ .

(4.9)

This noncommutative gauge theory is organised by the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra

\displaystyle\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}=\Big(\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N)),\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}=\mathrm{d}\big)\,,\,\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}=g\,[-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}-]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\,,\,\langle-,-\rangle_{\textrm{\tiny CS}}^{\star}\Big)\ ,

(4.10)

whose underlying cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})=\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N)),\mathrm{d}\big)
is the de Rham complex of differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
valued in the matrix Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(N)
. The cyclic structure of degree
-3
is given by the pairing of
\mathfrak{u}(N)
-valued differential forms in complementary degrees:

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\alpha^{+}\rangle^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny CS}}=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\alpha^{+})=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{+})\ ,

(4.11)

for
\alpha\in\{c,A\}
, with cyclicity ensured by the
\mathrm{ad}(\mathfrak{u}(N))
-invariance of the trace pairing
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}:\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes\mathfrak{u}(N)\longrightarrow\mathbbm{R}
.

For this strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra, the Maurer–Cartan theory from §
A.2
reproduces the standard curvature and Bianchi identity given in degree
2
and
3
respectively by

\begin{split}f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}&=\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A,A)=F_{A}^{\star}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathrm{d}_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}&=\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}})+\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A,f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}})=\nabla_{A}^{\star}F_{A}^{\star}=0\ ,\end{split}

(4.12)

and also the noncommutative Chern–Simons functional

\displaystyle S_{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}^{\star}[A]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A)\rangle_{\textrm{\tiny CS}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle A,\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A,A)\rangle_{\textrm{\tiny CS}}^{\star}\ ,

(4.13)

invariant under the star-gauge transformations
\delta_{c}^{\star}A=\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(c)+\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(c,A)
.

Batalin–Vilkovisky formalism.

The Batalin–Vilkovisky (BV) complex corresponding to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}
consists of BRST ghosts
c\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
, gauge fields
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
along with their antifields
c^{+}\in\Omega^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
and
A^{+}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
. The non-zero differentials
\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}:\Omega^{p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
are

\begin{split}\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(c)=\mathrm{d}c\quad,\quad\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A)=\mathrm{d}A\quad\mbox{,}\quad\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A^{+})=\mathrm{d}A^{+}\ ,\end{split}

(4.14a)

while the non-zero
2
-brackets
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}:\Omega^{p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\,\otimes\,\Omega^{q}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+q}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
are given by

\begin{split}\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(c_{1},c_{2})=g\,[c_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\quad,&\quad\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(c,A)=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(c,A^{+})=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\quad,&\quad\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A_{1},A_{2})=g\,[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(A,A^{+})=g\,[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\quad,&\quad\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(c,c^{+})=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ .\end{split}

(4.14b)

Consider now a superfield element
{\boldsymbol{A}}\in\mathsf{Fun}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))[1]\big)\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
. With the local coordinate functions
\vartheta^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p}}
on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))[1]
of degree
|\vartheta^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p}}|=1-p
, we can express the superfield as

{\boldsymbol{A}}=\vartheta\otimes c+\vartheta^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}+\vartheta^{\mu\nu}\otimes A^{+}_{\mu\nu}+\vartheta^{\mu\nu\rho}\otimes c^{+}_{\mu\nu\rho}\ .

(4.15)

Since
{\boldsymbol{A}}
is a degree
1
element by construction, we retrieve the full BV action functional from the Maurer–Cartan functional of
{\boldsymbol{A}}
as (see §
A.2
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}[{\boldsymbol{A}}]&=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A+\frac{g}{3!}\,A\wedge_{\star}[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+A^{+}\wedge_{\star}\nabla_{A}^{\star}\,c+\frac{g}{2}\,c^{+}\wedge_{\star}[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big)\ .\end{split}

(4.16)

In the commutative limit
t=0
, this reproduces the superfield Chern–Simons action functional of
[
39
]
entirely from the underlying
L_{\infty}
-structure.

The Seiberg–Witten map
[
69
]
gives rise to a quasi-isomorphism between a noncommutative gauge theory and a commutative gauge theory.
6
6

6

See e.g.
[
70
,
71
]
for recent explicit elucidations in the language of homotopy algebras.
The local BRST cohomology of standard Chern–Simons theory implies that any consistent deformation of commutative
\mathsf{U}(N)
Chern–Simons theory is trivial; in particular, the noncommutative deformation is trivial
[
72
,
73
]
. It follows that, under the Seiberg–Witten map, the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}
of noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(N)
Chern–Simons theory is quasi-isomorphic to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{CS}
of ordinary
\mathsf{U}(N)
Chern–Simons theory. This will account for some classical features of the homotopy double copy construction that we explain below. However, it does
not
imply that the corresponding quantum field theories are equivalent, and indeed we shall see that the amplitudes in the two theories are not the same.

4.3
Twisted homotopy factorisation

Colour-stripping of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory can be done in two equivalent ways, each of which will prove useful for our subsequent analysis. Following what we did in §
3.3
, we can first strip off the infinite-dimensional colour Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)
by extending the twist datum
\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}=\big(\tau_{1}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)},\tau_{2}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}\big)
to arbitrary degree differential forms, which by an abuse of notation we continue to denote with the same symbol. Thus the twist datum now
consists of the map
\tau_{1}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}:\mathfrak{u}(N)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})\big)
defined by

\displaystyle\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}_{1}(T^{a})=T^{a}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\ ,

(4.17a)

and the map
\tau_{2}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}:\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes\mathfrak{u}(N)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})\big)\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})\big)

defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tau_{2}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}(T^{a},T^{b})&=[T^{a},T^{b}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\otimes\cos\big(\tfrac{t}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\pounds_{\mu}\otimes\pounds_{\nu}\big)\\
&\quad\,+\mathrm{i}\,\{T^{a},T^{b}\}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\otimes\sin\big(\tfrac{t}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\pounds_{\mu}\otimes\pounds_{\nu}\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.17b)

By completely analogous calculations to those of §
3.3
, it is now straightforward to show that the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}
admits a homotopy factorization

\displaystyle\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}=\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}}\mathfrak{C}_{\Omega^{\bullet}}

(4.18)

into the twisted tensor product of the quadratic colour Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(N)
with the kinematical strict
C_{\infty}
-algebra

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\Omega^{\bullet}}=\Big(\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}),m_{1}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}=\mathrm{d}\big)\,,\,m_{2}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}=g\,\wedge\,,\,\langle-,-\rangle_{\Omega^{\bullet}}\Big)\ ,

(4.19)

with cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle_{\Omega^{\bullet}}
defined by integration of exterior products of forms in complementary degree. This is just the exterior algebra of differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
, which represents the infinitesimal diffeomorphism invariance of classical Chern–Simons theory.

Next we factorise
\mathfrak{C}_{\Omega^{\bullet}}
into a finite-dimensional kinematic vector space
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}
and a cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra encoding the trivalent interactions. As a graded vector space, we identify
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
with the tensor product
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}\otimes\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
by writing any one-form
\alpha=\alpha_{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
as
\alpha=e^{\mu}\otimes\alpha_{\mu}
, where
\alpha_{\mu}\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
and
e^{\mu}
is the natural basis of covectors in
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
relative to the rectangular coordinates
(x^{\mu})
.

At the level of homotopy algebras, the cyclic strict
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{\Omega^{\bullet}}
factorizes as a twisted tensor product

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\Omega^{\bullet}}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{Scal}

(4.20)

of the
1
-shifted exterior algebra

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}:=\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}[1]

(4.21)

with the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{Scal}
from §
2.1
for scalar field theory with underlying graded vector space (
3.33
) on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
. The twist datum
\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}=\big(\tau_{1}^{\Omega^{\bullet}},\tau_{2}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}\big)
is defined as follows. The map
\tau_{1}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}:\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathsf{End}(L)
is defined by

\displaystyle\tau_{1}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}(v)=(e^{\mu}\wedge v)\otimes\frac{\partial_{\mu}}{\square}\ ,

(4.22)

while
\tau_{2}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}:\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathsf{End}(L)\otimes\mathsf{End}(L)
is given as

\displaystyle\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}_{2}(v,w)=(v\wedge w)\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\ ,

(4.23)

for all
v,w\in\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}[1]
.

It is then straightforward to check that this twisting reproduces the differential:
m_{1}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}=m_{1}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}
; e.g. on degree
1
fields we find

\displaystyle m_{1}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}(e^{\mu}\otimes\alpha_{\mu})=(e^{\nu}\wedge e^{\mu})\otimes\mu_{1}^{\rm Scal}\big(\tfrac{\partial_{\nu}}{\square}\,\alpha_{\mu}\big)=(e^{\nu}\wedge e^{\mu})\otimes\partial_{\nu}\alpha_{\mu}=\mathrm{d}\alpha\ .

(4.24)

Similarly we reproduce the product:
m_{2}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}=m_{2}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}
; e.g. on degree
1
fields we find

\displaystyle m_{2}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}(e^{\mu}\otimes\alpha_{\mu},e^{\nu}\otimes\beta_{\nu})=(e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu})\otimes\mu_{2}^{\rm Scal}(\alpha_{\mu},\beta_{\nu})=(e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu})\otimes g\,\alpha_{\mu}\,\beta_{\nu}=g\,\alpha\wedge\beta\ .

(4.25)

Note, however, that due to the tensor product of the underlying graded vector spaces
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}[1]
and
L=\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-1]\oplus\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-2]
, the factorization (
4.20
) introduces some redundancy. For example, the cochain complex underlying the twisted tensor product is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-3]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

(4.26)

Finally, the cyclic structure is recovered by wedging forms in
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}
of complementary degrees and applying the Hodge duality operator
\mathrm{Tr}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}:\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{R}
in three dimensions. Then
\mathrm{Tr}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\langle-,-\rangle_{\rm Scal}
reproduces the inner product
\langle-,-\rangle_{\Omega^{\bullet}}
on
\mathfrak{C}_{\Omega^{\bullet}}
.

Altogether, we have shown that the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra of noncommutative Chern–Simons gauge theory admits the factorization

\displaystyle\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}=\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(4.27)

In particular, replacing
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}
with a copy of another colour Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(\bar{N})
and corresponding twisted tensor product yields the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
3.80
). This identifies the
d=3
binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory of §
3.7
as the zeroth copy of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory.

This last statement is perhaps more transparent if one notes that the factorization (
4.27
) can be equivalently written as the twisted tensor product

\displaystyle\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}\ ,

(4.28)

where the cyclic strict
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}
itself factorises as in
eq.

3.38
. In
eq.

4.28
the twist datum is defined in a completely analogous way to the twist datum above in terms of maps
\tau_{1}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}:\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes L\big)
and
\tau_{2}^{\Omega^{\bullet}}:(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}})^{\otimes 2}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes L\big)^{\otimes 2}
. For example, on degree
1
fields we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{1}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}(e^{\mu}\otimes\alpha_{\mu})&=(e^{\nu}\wedge e^{\mu})\otimes m_{1}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}\big(\tfrac{\partial_{\nu}}{\square}\,\alpha_{\mu}\big)=(e^{\nu}\wedge e^{\mu})\otimes\partial_{\nu}\alpha_{\mu}=\mathrm{d}\alpha\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}(e^{\mu}\otimes\alpha_{\mu},e^{\nu}\otimes\beta_{\nu})&=(e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu})\otimes\big(-m_{2}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}(\alpha_{\mu},\beta_{\nu})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=(e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu})\otimes g\,[\alpha_{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\beta_{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=g\,[\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\beta]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ .\end{split}

(4.29)

Compared to
eq.

4.27
, which is a factorisation into a colour Lie algebra and a kinematic
C_{\infty}
-algebra, in
eq.

4.28
the factorisation is into a kinematic vector space and a colour
C_{\infty}
-algebra.

4.4
Twisted colour-kinematics duality

Perturbative computations in noncommutative Chern–Simons theory are facilitated by using the factorisation (
4.28
) to decompose degree
1
fields as
A=e^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}
in terms of coordinate functions
A_{\mu}:\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{u}_{(}N)
, with bracket

\displaystyle[A_{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}B_{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=A_{\mu}\star B_{\nu}-B_{\nu}\star A_{\mu}

(4.30)

for
A_{\mu},B_{\nu}\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
. In this form, the three-gluon Feynman vertex can be computed directly by expanding the action functional (
4.7
) as

\displaystyle S_{\textrm{\tiny CS}}^{\star}[A]=\int\,\mathrm{d}^{3}x\ \epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\ \mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big(A_{\mu}\star\partial_{\nu}A_{\rho}+\frac{g}{3!}\,A_{\mu}\star[A_{\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{\rho}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big)\ ,

(4.31)

where
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}
is the Levi-Civita symbol in three dimensions with
\epsilon^{012}=1
.
It is given in terms of the structure constants of the infinite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)=\big(\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N)),[-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}-]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)
as

}\normalsize{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{73.51582pt}{-2.81447pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(4.32)

In the following we shall set up the perturbiner expansion for noncommutative Chern–Simons theory in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism, and following the discussion from §
3.5
we establish a twisted form of colour-kinematics duality compatible with the twisted colour-stripping.

Perturbiner solutions.

As Chern–Simons fields have no propagating degrees of freedom, all currents vanish on-shell and there are no interactions; in particular, the pure gauge theory has no non-trivial S-matrix elements. From the homotopy algebraic perspective, this is an easy consequence of the fact that the de Rham cohomology of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
is trivial:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf H}^{0}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})=\ker(\mathrm{d})\otimes\mathfrak{u}(N)\quad,\quad{\sf H}^{3}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})=\ker(\delta)\otimes\mathfrak{u}(N)\quad,\quad{\sf H}^{1}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})&={\sf H}^{2}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})=0\ ,\end{split}

(4.33)

where
\delta=\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\mathrm{d}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
is the codifferential corresponding to the exterior differential
\mathrm{d}
and the Hodge duality operator
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
induced by the Minkowski metric. Correlation functions with external states lying in
{\sf H}^{1}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})
are thus always trivially zero.

As we did in §
3.6
, below we will construct the off-shell action functional of the double copied theory using Maurer–Cartan theory, so we should first establish that colour-kinematics duality holds off-shell from the homotopy algebraic perspective.
Off-shell correlation functions are obtained by projecting onto “harmonic” states
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})=\ker(\square)
rather than the minimal model
[
74
,
75
,
13
]
, where
\square=\mathrm{d}\,\delta+\delta\,\mathrm{d}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is the Hodge–d’Alembertian operator acting on forms; in Minkowski signature this is a bigger space than
{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})
. Correlation functions on this space will be non-zero as, unlike the projection to the cohomology, the space of harmonic forms contains configurations which are not pure gauge and hence can propagate.

Chern–Simons perturbation theory requires a “Hodge inverse” of the differential
\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}=\mathrm{d}
, which is constructed using
\square
together with the Hodge–Kodaira decomposition (
A.35
).
We look for a contracting homotopy
\mathsf{h}
satisfying a Hodge–Kodaira decomposition for projection
{\sf p}_{\ker(\square)}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})
onto harmonic states:

\mathbbm{1}={\sf p}_{\ker(\square)}+\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}\circ\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{h}\circ\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}\ .

(4.34)

The contracting homotopy is the partial inverse of the differential
\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}
on the space of pure gauge configurations
\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{d})\oplus\mathrm{im}(\delta)
, extended trivially to all of
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
by

\begin{split}\mathsf{h}:\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{d})\oplus\mathrm{im}(\delta)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ker(\mathsf{h})=\ker(\square)\ .\end{split}

(4.35)

It can be constructed from the partial inverse
\mathsf{D}
of the d’Alembertian
\square
on the subspace of pure gauge configurations by extending it trivially to
\ker(\square)
:

\mathsf{D}\big|_{\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{d})\oplus\mathrm{im}(\delta)}=\frac{1}{\square}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ker(\mathsf{D})=\ker(\square)\ .

(4.36)

A choice of contracting homotopy
{\sf h}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
of degree
-1
completing the Hodge–Kodaira decomposition (
4.34
) is then

\mathsf{h}=\delta\circ\mathsf{D}\ .

(4.37)

Acting on gauge fields and ghost antifields, the maps
{\sf h}^{(1)}:\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
and
{\sf h}^{(3)}:\Omega^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
are respectively given by

\displaystyle{\sf h}^{(1)}(A)=\frac{\partial_{\mu}}{\square}\,A^{\mu}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{\sf h}^{(3)}(c^{+})_{\mu\nu}=\frac{\partial^{\rho}}{\square}\,c^{+}_{\mu\nu\rho}\ .

(4.38)

We set
{\sf h}^{(1)}(A)=0
, which imposes the Lorenz gauge
\delta A=\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
. Acting on antifields in degree
2
, the contracting homotopy is then given by the Feynman propagator for Chern–Simons theory:
{\sf h}^{(2)}=C_{\rm F}:\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
where

C_{\rm F}(A^{+})_{\rho}=\epsilon_{\rho\sigma\alpha}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\sigma}\,\frac{\partial^{\alpha}}{\square}\,A^{+}_{\mu\nu}\ .

(4.39)

Indeed, using these formulas, and the fact that the distribution
\frac{1}{\square}
commutes with the operators
\mathrm{d}
,
\delta
and
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
, we verify the projector onto harmonic forms fits into
eq.

4.34
.

Given a perturbiner element
A(i)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})
corresponding to the ‘
i
-th’ gluon, we look for a quasi-isomorphism
\psi_{n}:\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))
which is again given by the
L_{\infty}
-recursion relations.
We work in terms of word combinatorics, where an ordered word
w=k_{1}\cdots k_{n}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
represents
n
external gluons.
The pullback to ordered words on
n
letters has the physical interpretation of performing a colour ordering expansion, as is often done in Yang–Mills theory.
The Berends–Giele currents
J_{\mu}:\mathscr{W}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{u}(N)
are the coefficients of the plane wave expansions

\psi_{n}\big(A(1),\dots,A(n)\big)=e^{\mu}\otimes J_{\mu}(w)\,\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{1\cdots n}\cdot x}\ \in\ \Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\ ,

(4.40)

where
p_{1\cdots n}:=p_{1}+\cdots+p_{n}
. The currents
J(w):=e^{\mu}\otimes J_{\mu}(w)
are coclosed in
\ker({\sf h}^{(1)})
,
p_{w}\cdot J(w)=0
, from the way in which we have built the contracting homotopy
{\sf h}
.

Since
\square
is a second order differential operator, the star-commutator of two currents is not generally in
\ker(\square)
.
Thus upon using the recursion relations from
eqs.

A.37b
and
A.37d
, successive application of the
2
-bracket
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}
of the full theory move us out of the harmonic states. Similarly, the codifferential can be expressed as
\delta=\frac{\partial}{\partial e^{\mu}}\otimes\partial^{\mu}
and so is a second order differential operator of degree
-1
. Hence the
2
-bracket
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}
of two currents is also not generally coclosed, but this is restored upon following with the Chern–Simons propagator
\mathsf{h}^{(2)}=C_{\rm F}
.

The recursion relations for the quasi-isomorphism give

\begin{split}J(w)&=-\frac{g}{2}\,\sum_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,e^{\rho}\otimes\big((C_{\rm F})^{\mu\nu}_{\rho}\circ\,[J_{\mu}(w_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}J_{\nu}(w_{2})]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{n}^{\circ\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}\big(A(1),\dots,A(n)\big)&=\frac{g}{2}\,\sum_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,\big(e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu}\big)\otimes\big({\sf p}_{\ker(\square)}\circ[J_{\mu}(w_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}J_{\nu}(w_{2})]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{1\cdots n}\cdot x}\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.41)

The images of the
n
-brackets
\mu_{n}^{\circ\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}
are coclosed in
\ker({\sf h}^{(2)})
, by our construction of the contracting homotopy
{\sf h}
.
The final projection to the harmonic states
{\sf p}_{\ker(\square)}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{CS}_{\star})
also enforces momentum conservation
s_{1\cdots n}=0
, where
s_{w}
are the Mandelstam variables.

The colour ordered partial amplitudes are given by substitution into the cyclic structure on the harmonic states. This gives

\mathcal{M}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}_{n}(1,\ldots,n)=\langle A(1),\mu^{\circ\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}_{n-1}(A(2),\ldots,A(n))\,\rangle^{\star}_{\circ{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}

(4.42)

for the
n
-gluon amplitudes.

Colour-kinematics duality.

With the Feynman propagator from
eq.

4.39
, and understanding star-commutators as living in
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)
, the recursion relations can be written as

\begin{split}J_{\rho}(w)&=-\frac{g}{2}\,\sum_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,\delta_{[\alpha\rho]}^{\mu\nu}\,\frac{p_{w}^{\alpha}}{s_{w}}\,[J_{\mu}(w_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}J_{\nu}(w_{2})]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\\[4.0pt]
&=-\frac{g}{2s_{w}}\,\sum_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,\Big(\big(p_{w_{2}}\cdot J^{a}(p_{w_{1}})^{\sharp}\big)\,J^{b}_{\rho}(p_{w_{2}})-J^{a}_{\rho}(p_{w_{1}})\,\big(p_{w_{1}}\cdot J^{b}(p_{w_{2}})^{\sharp}\big)\Big)\\
&\hskip 284.52756pt\times F^{abc}(p_{w_{1}},p_{w_{2}},p_{w})\,T^{c}\ ,\end{split}

(4.43)

where
\delta^{\mu\nu}_{[\alpha\rho]}:=\delta^{\mu}_{\alpha}\,\delta^{\nu}_{\rho}-\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\,\delta^{\nu}_{\alpha}
, and we have decomposed the Berends–Giele currents as tensor products
J_{\mu}(w)=J_{\mu}^{a}(p_{w})\otimes\,e^{a}_{p_{w}}
using the momentum space basis of the infinite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)
introduced in
eq.

3.15
. The superscript
♯
indicates the operation which sends a covector in
(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}
to its dual vector in
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
.

The coefficients
J^{a}(p_{w})
can be thought of in terms of a basis of covectors
\varepsilon_{p_{w}}\in(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}
, which are dual to gluon polarisation vectors
\varepsilon_{p_{w}}^{\sharp}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
. In Lorenz gauge, a given momentum
p_{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{1,2}
is orthogonal to the corresponding polarisation vector:
p_{w}\cdot\varepsilon_{p_{w}}^{\sharp}=0
, corresponding to projection to states lying in
\ker(\delta)
.
These covectors on
\mathbb{R}^{1,2}
naturally form an infinite-dimensional Lie algebra under the bracket

[\varepsilon_{p_{w_{1}}},\varepsilon_{p_{w_{2}}}]_{\mathfrak{diff}(\mathbb{R}^{1,2})}:=\big(\varepsilon_{p_{w_{1}}}^{\sharp}\cdot p_{w_{2}}\big)\,\varepsilon_{p_{w_{2}}}-\big(\varepsilon^{\sharp}_{p_{w_{2}}}\cdot p_{w_{1}}\big)\,\varepsilon_{p_{w_{1}}}\ .

(4.44)

In particular, the bracket of two covectors is transverse to the total momentum they carry, that is,
p_{12}\cdot[\varepsilon_{p_{1}},\varepsilon_{p_{2}}]^{\sharp}_{\mathfrak{diff}(\mathbb{R}^{1,2})}=0
. This is recognised as the bracket of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{diff}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
of infinitesimal diffeomorphisms of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
. In Lorenz gauge, this restricts to the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{vol}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
of volume-preserving diffeomorphisms, i.e. divergence-free vector fields on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
.

We can therefore express the recursion relations for the Berends–Giele currents in terms of a twisted tensor product
\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}}\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{vol}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
of two Lie algebras, which defines a symmetric bracket operation

\begin{split}\llbracket J(w_{1}),J(w_{2})\rrbracket_{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}^{\star}:=&\ e^{\rho}\otimes\delta^{\mu\nu}_{[\alpha\rho]}\,p^{\alpha}_{w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,[J_{\mu}(w_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}J_{\nu}(w_{2})]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\\[4.0pt]
=&\ [J^{a}(p_{w_{1}}),J^{b}(p_{w_{2}})]_{\mathfrak{diff}(\mathbb{R}^{1,2})}\otimes[e^{a}_{p_{w_{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}e^{b}_{p_{w_{2}}}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ .\end{split}

(4.45)

The intertwining of momenta in
eq.

4.45
simply reflects the twisted factorization of colour degrees of freedom in
eq.

4.27
, and the related absence of full diffeomorphism invariance in the Moyal–Weyl deformation of Chern–Simons gauge theory.

The recursion relations thus become

\begin{split}J(w)&=-\frac{g}{2}\,\sum_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,\frac{\llbracket J(w_{1}),J(w_{2})\rrbracket^{\star}_{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}}{s_{w}}\ ,\end{split}

(4.46)

with currents
J(w)\in\ker(\mathsf{h}^{(1)})
.
At the level of amplitudes, we scatter harmonic gluon states
A(1)=e^{\mu}\otimes e^{a}_{p_{1}}\otimes A^{a}_{\mu}(1)
with
A^{a}_{\mu}(1)\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
satisfying
\square A^{a}_{\mu}(1)=0
. Then the
n
-point colour ordered partial amplitude is written as

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(1,w)&=\Big\langle A(1)\>,\>{\frac{g}{2}\,\sum\limits_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,{\sf p}_{\ker(\square)}\big([J(w_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}J(w_{2})]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{w}\cdot x}\big)}\Big\rangle^{\star}_{\circ{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}\ .\end{split}

(4.47)

The projection operator enforces
p_{w}^{2}=0
, while
p_{1}^{2}=0
already for the first state.
Altogether one finds

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(1,w)&=\frac{g}{2}\,\sum_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\,F^{abc}(p_{w_{1}},p_{w_{2}},p_{1})\,J^{a}_{\mu}(w_{1})\,J^{b}_{\nu}(w_{2})\,A^{c}_{\rho}(1)\ (2\pi)^{3}\,\delta(p_{1w})\ .\end{split}

(4.48)

The full amplitude is obtained by summing over planar ordered permutations of
n-1
gluons, and performing the recursion (
4.46
) for the currents
J(w)
.

By running through the same argument from §
3.5
for the adjoint scalar theory, this demonstrates a twisted form of colour-kinematics duality for currents of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory, between the Lie algebras underlying volume-preserving diffeomorphisms of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
and noncommutative gauge transformations. In the commutative limit
t=0
, this identifies
\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{vol}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
as the kinematic Lie algebra underlying true (off-shell) colour-kinematics duality in ordinary Chern–Simons theory
[
39
]
. Furthermore, from the perspective of
eq.

4.45
, the zeroth copy construction corresponds to replacing the kinematic factors
J(w)
with a set of twisted colour factors
C_{\bar{\star}}(w)
for the second rigid Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(\bar{N})
of the
d=3
binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory from §
3.7
.

4.5
Homotopy double copy

The twisted colour-kinematics duality discussed in §
4.4
makes it clear that, in noncommutative gauge theories of the type considered in this paper, the double copy involves a replacement of the full
twisted
colour-stripping by kinematical factors.
From the factorization (
4.27
), the double copy prescription is clear: the homotopy double copy construction replaces the twisted tensor product with the colour factor
\mathfrak{u}(N)
by the twisted tensor product with another copy of the kinematical factor
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}[1]
, which gives the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(4.49)

The second kinematical twist datum is defined by maps
\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}_{1}:\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes L\big)
and
\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}_{2}:\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes L\big)\otimes\mathsf{End}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes L\big)
.

Most notably, this construction asserts the general statement that double copies of noncommutative gauge theories are not deformed and coincide with their commutative counterparts. In particular, the double copy theory organised by the
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
4.49
) coincides with that obtained in
[
39
]
from a more physical perspective. In what follows we will unravel some further details about this non-local higher-spin theory, which involves two copies of the volume-preserving diffeomorphism algebra
\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{vol}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
.

In the following we regard the vector space
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}\otimes\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})^{*}\otimes\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
as the tensor product
\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}):=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})\otimes_{\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})}\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
of
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
with itself, considered as a module over
\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
; we can think of its elements as differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
valued in the exterior algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
. For example, an element
H\in\Omega^{1,1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
can be decomposed as
H=e^{\bar{\mu}}\otimes H_{\bar{\mu}}
relative to a basis
e^{\bar{\mu}}
corresponding to a rectangular coordinate system
(x^{\bar{\mu}})
, with
H_{\bar{\mu}}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
. We may then further expand
H_{\bar{\mu}}=e^{\mu}\otimes H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}
relative to the given basis
e^{\mu}
, where
H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
; we regard
x^{\mu}
and
x^{\bar{\mu}}
as independent variables, so that in particular the corresponding partial derivatives
\partial_{\mu}
and
\partial_{\bar{\mu}}
commute. This enables the definition of a tensor product differential
\mathrm{d}\otimes\mathrm{d}
, sending
\Omega^{1,1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
to
\Omega^{2,2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})

The graded vector space
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes L
underlying the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}}
has
16
homogeneous components. Any zero differential appearing in the redundant part of
eq.

4.26
yields a zero differential in the double copy, making the cochain complex of
\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}}
“diagonal” in the sense that its non-trivial part is

\hbox to370.12pt{\vbox to18.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 185.05562pt\lower-8.97974pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-185.05562pt}{-2.82pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{85.33324pt}{2.86612pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ .

(4.50)

For example, on fields of degree
1
, the differential
\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}(e^{\bar{\mu}}\otimes e^{\mu}\otimes H_{\bar{\mu}\mu})&=(e^{\bar{\nu}}\wedge e^{\bar{\mu}})\otimes m_{1}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\big(e^{\mu}\otimes\tfrac{\partial_{\bar{\nu}}}{\square}\,H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=(e^{\bar{\nu}}\wedge e^{\bar{\mu}})\otimes(e^{\nu}\wedge e^{\mu})\otimes\frac{1}{\square}\,\partial_{\nu}\,\partial_{\bar{\nu}}H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\end{split}

(4.51)

for
H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
, because we treat the left and right factors of the kinematical vector space
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}
as independent.

The
2
-bracket
\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}:\Omega^{p,q}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-i]\otimes\Omega^{r,s}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-j]\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+r,q+s}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[-i-j]
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}}
in addition acts non-trivially on “off-diagonal” fields.
It is easily identified as the map which scales the tensor product
\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\wedge\otimes\ \wedge
of independent exterior products on the left and right kinematical vector spaces. For example, the bracket of two fields of degree
1
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(e^{\bar{\mu}}\otimes e^{\mu}\otimes H_{\bar{\mu}\mu},e^{\bar{\nu}}\otimes e^{\nu}\otimes H^{\prime}_{\bar{\nu}\nu})&=(e^{\bar{\mu}}\wedge e^{\bar{\nu}})\otimes m_{2}^{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}(e^{\mu}\otimes H_{\bar{\mu}\mu},e^{\nu}\otimes H^{\prime}_{\bar{\nu}\nu})\\[4.0pt]
&=(e^{\bar{\mu}}\wedge e^{\bar{\nu}})\otimes(e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu})\otimes\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\,H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\,H^{\prime}_{\bar{\nu}\nu}\ .\end{split}

(4.52)

The cyclic structure is obtained by composing the tensor product of Hodge duality operators with wedge products in complementary degrees in each kinematic factor:

\displaystyle\langle-,-\rangle_{\widehat{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}}:=\mathrm{Tr}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\mathrm{Tr}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\langle-,-\rangle_{\rm Scal}\ .

(4.53)

Altogether, this makes the twisted tensor product (
4.49
) into a cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

In Maurer–Cartan theory for the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}}
, the underlying homogeneous spaces of the “diagonal” cochain complex (
4.50
) contain all relevant data of the double copy field theory, because only that part of the complex is non-trivial. The Maurer–Cartan equation

\displaystyle\widehat{f}^{\,{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}_{H}:=\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(H)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(H,H)=0

(4.54)

for
H\in\Omega^{1,1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
gives the equation of motion

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\Big(\frac{1}{\square}\,\partial_{\bar{\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}H_{\bar{\rho}\rho}+\frac{\kappa}{4}\,H_{\bar{\nu}\nu}\,H_{\bar{\rho}\rho}\Big)=0\ .

(4.55)

These are the variational equations for the non-local classical action functional which is extracted from the Maurer–Cartan functional

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{S}_{\textrm{\tiny CS}}[H]&=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle H,\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(H)\rangle_{\widehat{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle H,\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(H,H)\rangle_{\widehat{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}}\\[4.0pt]
&=\int\,\mathrm{d}^{3}x\ \epsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\Big(H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\,\frac{1}{\square}\,\partial_{\bar{\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}H_{\bar{\rho}\rho}+\frac{\kappa}{6}\,H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\,H_{\bar{\nu}\nu}\,H_{\bar{\rho}\rho}\Big)\ .\end{split}

(4.56)

This theory is invariant under the variations
\widehat{\delta}_{C}H=\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(C)+\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(H,H)
for a gauge parameter
C\in\Omega^{0,0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})\simeq\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
, which reads explicitly as

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{C}H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}=\frac{1}{\square}\,\partial_{\bar{\mu}}\,\partial_{\mu}C+\frac{\kappa}{2}\,C\,H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}\ .

(4.57)

The Noether identity corresponding to this local gauge symmetry is the off-shell Bianchi identity for the curvature in
eq.

4.54
:
\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(\widehat{f}^{\,{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}_{H})+\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}(H,\widehat{f}^{\,{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}_{H})=0
.
