\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(4.58)

The Maurer–Cartan functional (
4.56
) matches precisely with the non-local gauge-fixed action functional of
[
39
]
, which was obtained by identifying
H_{\bar{\mu}\mu}=A_{\bar{\mu}}\otimes A_{\mu}
as a tensor product of fields in momentum space, and writing down its propagator and interaction vertex by double copying the Chern–Simons kinematic numerators in Lorenz gauge. In our approach, the result of the double copy procedure applied to Chern–Simons theory is immediate, with its local gauge symmetry automatically identified, and with the gauge-fixing prescription needed for the colour-kinematics duality of §
4.4
manifest in the homotopy double copy construction without additional input.

Our perspective also allows for a simple construction of the superspace formulation of the double copy in
[
39
]
that includes all bosonic fields and ghosts: Applying the Maurer–Cartan functional to a superfield
{\boldsymbol{H}}\in\mathsf{Fun}\big(\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})[1]\big)\otimes\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
yields the BV action functional

\widehat{S}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}[{\boldsymbol{H}}]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{H}},\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}\,\textrm{ext}}({\boldsymbol{H}})\rangle_{\widehat{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}\,\textrm{ext}}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{H}},\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}\,\textrm{ext}}({\boldsymbol{H}},{\boldsymbol{H}})\rangle_{\widehat{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}\,\textrm{ext}}\ ,

(4.59)

where
{\boldsymbol{H}}=\overline{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{A}}
is a double copy of two Chern–Simons superfields (
4.15
) and the notation is defined in §
A.2
.
Thus the homotopy double copy framework offers a more systematic and rigorous method of tackling the problem.

4.6
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
Chern–Simons theory as a double copy

Similarly to the adjoint scalar theory from §
3.4
, the rank one limit
N=1
of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory is an interacting theory of photons with no non-trivial commutative counterpart. The noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Chern–Simons gauge theory on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
is defined by the action functional

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}_{\star}(1)}[A]=\int\,\frac{1}{2}\,A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A+\frac{\kappa}{6}\,A\wedge_{\star}A\wedge_{\star}A\ ,

(4.60)

for
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
. This theory is organised by the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}(1)}=\Big(\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}),\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}_{\star}(1)}=\mathrm{d}\big)\,,\,\widehat{\mu}_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}_{\star}(1)}=\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\,[-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}-]_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}\,,\,\langle-,-\rangle_{\Omega^{\bullet}}\Big)\ ,

(4.61)

whose underlying cochain complex is just the de Rham complex of differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
.

As the notation suggests, we regard this noncommutative gauge theory without colour degrees of freedom itself as a double copy of a field theory with colour symmetry. This is analogous to the rank one limits
N=\bar{N}=1
of the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory of §
3.7
, which results in the homotopy double copy theory of §
3.6
. The two equivalent twisted factorizations (
4.27
) and (
4.28
) offer two (equivalent) perspectives on the origins of this double copied theory, which corroborates the old suggestions, discussed in
section

1
, that noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge theories realise models of gravity in certain senses, despite the absence of propagating spin two states.

The twisted factorisation (
4.28
) for
N=1
is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}(1)}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{C}_{\rm\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)}\ .

(4.62)

We use the factorization (
3.38
), together with the interpretation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
as a kinematical factor with vector space
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}=\mathbbm{R}
from §
3.4
, to write the factorisation of the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra of
\mathsf{U}(1)
noncommutative Chern–Simons theory as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}(1)}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(4.63)

The underlying cochain complex in this factorization is again given by
eq.

4.26
.
Comparing
eqs.

3.50
and
4.63
, and recalling the colour-kinematics duality of §
3.5
, we conclude that the noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Chern–Simons theory is a double copy of the
d=3
adjoint scalar theory of §
3.4
for any colour algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

To better understand this statement, we note that the construction of §
4.3
shows that
ordinary
Chern–Simons gauge theory based on a quadratic Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is organised by a cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{CS}
which admits the factorization

\displaystyle\mathfrak{CS}=\mathfrak{g}\otimes\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ ,

(4.64)

because the colour-stripping is not twisted in the commutative case.
Instead of the double copy (
3.69
) of the adjoint scalar theory with itself that we considered in §
3.6
, we can take its double copy with commutative Chern–Simons theory for any gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, which involves the kinematical vector space
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}
and leads to the double copy prescription of
eq.

4.63
.

We denote this double copy operation symbolically by

\displaystyle\text{$\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)$ Chern--Simons}\ =\ \text{Adjoint Scalar}\ \otimes\ \text{Ordinary Chern--Simons}

(4.65)

with reducible fields
A_{\mu}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)}=\phi\otimes A_{\mu}^{\textrm{\tiny CS}}
. The twisted tensor product construction of
eq.

4.63
combines the kinematic numerators of the adjoint scalar theory, based on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
, with those of commutative Chern–Simons theory, based on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{vol}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
.

Alternatively, again with the understanding of
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
as a kinematical Lie algebra analogously to §
3.4
, the twisted factorisation (
4.27
) for
N=1
is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{CS}_{\star}(1)}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)}}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(4.66)

Comparing
eqs.

4.64
and
4.66
, and recalling the colour-kinematics duality of §
4.4
, we conclude now that the noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Chern–Simons theory is a double copy of the commutative Chern–Simons theory for any gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with the adjoint scalar theory. This perspective is very natural, as the propagators in the commutative and noncommutative gauge theories are the same while the three-point interaction vertex is copied as

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\,g\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}\,f^{abc}\longrightarrow-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\kappa}{2}\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}\,F(k,p,q)

(4.67)

in terms of the structure constants of the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
. As the twisted tensor product operations commute, i.e. formally
\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)}}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)}}\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes_{\tau^{\Omega^{\bullet}}}
, the two double copy operations lead to the same theory (
4.65
), as expected on heuristic grounds.

Diffeomorphism invariance.

The rank one limit of the brackets (
4.45
) is given by

\begin{split}\llbracket J(w_{1}),J(w_{2})\rrbracket^{\star}_{{\textrm{\tiny CS}}}=[\varepsilon_{p_{w_{1}}},\varepsilon_{p_{w_{2}}}]_{\mathfrak{diff}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})}\otimes 2\,\mathrm{i}\,\sin\big(\tfrac{t}{2}\,p_{w_{1}}\cdot\theta\,p_{w_{2}}\big)\,e_{p_{w_{3}}}\ \delta(p_{w_{1}}+p_{w_{2}}-p_{w_{3}})\ .\end{split}

(4.68)

This infinite-dimensional Lie algebra is generated by the divergence-free vector fields

\varepsilon_{\mu}(p)=\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p\cdot x}\,\Pi_{\mu\nu}(p)\,\partial^{\nu}

(4.69)

for a transverse projection tensor
\Pi_{\mu\nu}(p)
with
\Pi_{\mu\nu}(p)\,p^{\mu}=0
.
Their deformed Lie bracket is

\begin{split}[\varepsilon_{\mu}(p_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\varepsilon_{\nu}(p_{2})]_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}=\int\,\mathrm{d}^{3}p_{3}\ {\tt F}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\ \varepsilon_{\rho}(p_{3})\ ,\end{split}

(4.70)

where

\begin{split}{\tt F}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})&=2\,\mathrm{i}\,\sin\big(\tfrac{t}{2}\,p_{1}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}\big)\,\Pi_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(p_{1})\,\epsilon^{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\rho}\,\Pi_{\nu^{\prime}\nu}(p_{2})\ \delta(p_{{1}}+p_{{2}}-p_{{3}})\ .\end{split}

(4.71)

This is the twisted tensor product of the Lie algebra of infinitesimal volume-preserving diffeomorphisms of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
with the abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(1)
.
In the semi-classical limit, it is the Poisson–Lie algebra of symplectic diffeomorphisms of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
.

In the commutative case, a double copy theory which is mapped from a gauge theory should be invariant under diffeomorphisms.
Here we find that
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
Chern–Simons theory, interpreted as a double copied theory, realises a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{vol}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})
consisting of deformed symplectic diffeomorphisms of spacetime, in the sense of
[
32
]
.
This vindicates our interpretation of the
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
Chern–Simons theory as a genuine double copy, but with reduced diffeomorphism symmetry at the amplitude level.

5
Noncommutative Yang–Mills theory

5.1
The
L_{\infty}
-structure of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory

Consider the quadratic Lie algebra
\big(\mathfrak{u}(N),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)},\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)
with the usual normalisation of the trace of generators
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(T^{a}\,T^{b})=\delta^{ab}
. We work on
d
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
, with Hodge duality operator denoted
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}:\Omega^{p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\longrightarrow\Omega^{d-p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
. The corresponding codifferential
\delta:\Omega^{p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\longrightarrow\Omega^{p-1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
is given by
\delta=(-1)^{d\,p+1}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\mathrm{d}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
.

The classical action functional for standard
\mathsf{U}(N)
noncommutative Yang–Mills theory on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
is given by

S^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}[A]=\frac{1}{2}\,\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big(F_{A}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,F_{A}^{\star}\big)\ ,

(5.1)

with gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
and noncommutative field strength
F_{A}^{\star}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
given by
eq.

4.8
, where
g
is the Yang–Mills coupling constant. The noncommutative Yang–Mills functional is invariant under infinitesimal star-gauge transformations
\delta^{\star}_{c}A=\mathrm{d}c+g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}
with
c\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
.

This noncommutative gauge theory is organised into a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra

\mathfrak{YM}_{\star}=\Big(\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{YM}_{\star})\,,\,\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}\,,\,\mu_{3}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}\,,\,\langle-,-\rangle_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}^{\star}\Big)\ .

(5.2)

The underlying cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{YM_{\star}})
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))\xrightarrow{\ \mathrm{d}\ }\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},&\mathfrak{u}(N))[-1]\\
&\xrightarrow{\ \delta\,\mathrm{d}\ }\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]\xrightarrow{\ \delta\ }\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-3]\ ,\end{split}

(5.3a)

which identifies the differential as
\mu_{1}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(c)=\mathrm{d}c
on ghosts
c
in
\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
, as
\mu_{1}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A)=\delta\,\mathrm{d}A
on gauge fields
A
in
\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-1]
, and as
\mu_{1}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A^{+})=\delta A^{+}
on antifields
A^{+}
in
\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]
.

Together with the ghost antifields
c^{+}\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-3]
, the non-zero
2
-brackets are given by

\begin{split}\mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(c_{1},c_{2})=g\,[c_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,&\ \mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(c,A)=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,\ \mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(c,A^{+})=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(c,c^{+})=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,&\ \mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A,A^{+})=g\,[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A_{1},A_{2})=g\,\big(\delta\,[A_{1}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.3b)

The single non-zero higher bracket
\mu_{3}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}:\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N)))[-1]^{\otimes 3}\longrightarrow\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-3]
acts as

\begin{split}\mu_{3}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})&=g^{2}\,\big(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[A_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{3}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[A_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[A_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{1}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt+\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[A_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.3c)

The cyclic structure of
L_{\infty}
-degree
-3
is given by the Hodge inner product of differential forms in the same exterior degree:

\langle\alpha,\alpha^{+}\rangle_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}^{\star}=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\alpha^{+})=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(\alpha\wedge\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\alpha^{+})\ ,

(5.3d)

for
\alpha\in\{c,A\}
.

Applying the Maurer–Cartan theory from §
A.2
for the cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
5.2
), the Maurer–Cartan curvature
f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]
from
eq.

A.17
reads

f^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{A}=\mu_{1}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A)+\tfrac{1}{2!}\,\mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A,A)+\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\mu_{3}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(A,A,A)=-\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\nabla_{A}^{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F^{\star}_{A}\ ,

(5.4)

where
\nabla_{A}^{\star}:\Omega^{p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is the covariant derivative given by
eq.

4.9
.
The Maurer–Cartan equation
f^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{A}=0
is therefore equivalent to the noncommutative Yang–Mills equations
\nabla^{\star}_{A}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F^{\star}_{A}=0
.

The Maurer–Cartan–Bianchi identity in
\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-3]
is the Noether identity for the star-gauge symmetry
\delta_{c}^{\star}A=\mu_{1}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(c)+\mu_{2}^{\star\textrm{\tiny YM}}(c,A)
of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory, which is satisfied off-shell. It expresses the fact that the Maurer–Cartan curvature
f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}
is covariantly constant for the Maurer–Cartan covariant derivative

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}&=\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}})+\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(A,f_{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}})\\[4.0pt]
&=-\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\nabla^{\star}_{A}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}f^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{A}=\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,(\nabla^{\star}_{A})^{2}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F^{\star}_{A}=\ast_{{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,[F_{A}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,F_{A}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}=0\ ,\end{split}

(5.5)

which follows directly from the symmetry properties of the four-form
[F_{A}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}
in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)
.

Finally, the Maurer–Cartan functional

\begin{split}S^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}[A]&=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big\langle A,\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(A)\big\rangle^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}+\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\big\langle A,\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(A,A)\big\rangle^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}+\tfrac{1}{4!}\,\big\langle A,\mu_{3}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(A,A,A)\big\rangle^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}\end{split}

(5.6)

recovers the noncommutative Yang–Mills functional from
eq.

5.1
.

Batalin–Vilkovisky formalism.

Following the same steps as in
section

4.2
, the superspace extension of the noncommutative Yang–Mills functional (
5.1
) yields the BV action functional
7
7

7

See e.g.
[
76
]
for the BRST extension including the antighost of the ghost field
c
and the Nakanishi–Lautrup auxiliary field.

\displaystyle S_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}[{\boldsymbol{A}}]=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,F_{A}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,F^{\star}_{A}+A^{+}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\nabla_{A}^{\star}\,c+\frac{g}{2}\,c^{+}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big)\ .

(5.7)

In contrast to the noncommutative Chern–Simons theory of
section

4
, the noncommutative deformation here corresponds to a non-trivial local BRST cohomology class and so it is non-trivial
[
72
,
73
]
: the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{YM}_{\star}
is
not
quasi-isomorphic to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{YM}
of ordinary Yang–Mills theory. This explains why the double copy construction of
section

4
was so straightforward; for noncommutative Yang–Mills theory we have to work harder.

Colour ordering and decomposition.

A common way to organise
n
-point amplitude calculations in gauge theories is by summing over inequivalent orderings of colour factors, which yields reduction formulas from
n!
down to
(n-1)!
inequivalent amplitudes. Similarly to the biadjoint scalar theory from
section

2.2
, tree-level scattering amplitudes of gluons are organised in terms of gauge-invariant partial amplitudes and colour structures as
[
77
]

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}(p,\zeta,a)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}/\mathbbm{Z}_{n}}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(T^{a_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots T^{a_{\sigma(n)}})\ \mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}\big(\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(n)\big)\ .

(5.8)

Each Yang–Mills partial amplitude appears twice due to the ordering properties

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}(1,\dots,n-1,n)=\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}(n,1,\dots,n-1)=(-1)^{n}\,\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}(n,n-1,\dots,1)\ ,

(5.9)

which enables one to restrict the sum to planar orderings as

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}(p,\zeta,a)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}/\mathbbm{Z}_{n}\rtimes\mathbbm{Z}_{2}}\,C(\sigma)\ \mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}\big(\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(n)\big)\ .

(5.10)

In
[
78
,
79
]
it is shown that noncommutative gauge theories still respect the colour decomposition.
As we have seen already in
eq.

3.83
, the structure constants
F^{abc}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
of
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)
can be decomposed to give the three-point vertex for noncommutative Yang–Mills theory in the form

\begin{split}\hbox to404.23pt{\vbox to78.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.67787pt\lower-40.43567pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(5.11a)

where for simplicity we suppress momentum conserving delta-functions.
Note that the two summands are considered as parts of different colour ordered amplitudes.
Similarly, the four-point vertex can be decomposed into a sum over inequivalent orderings with phase factors dressing the summands as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{70.603pt}{-2.67415pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{109.13539pt}{-18.83353pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{111.49257pt}{-38.6698pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

} \normalsize
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(5.11b)

Since tree-level amplitudes involve only planar diagrams, colour ordered amplitudes in noncommutative Yang–Mills theory factorise with the appropriate phase factor
[
78
,
79
]
.
Given an ordered word
w=k_{1}\cdots k_{n-1}k_{n}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
labelling colour, polarisations and momenta, for rank
N>1
one finds that the noncommutative
n
-point Yang–Mills partial amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(w)
are related to the
commutative

n
-point partial amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}(w)
through the simple relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(w)

\displaystyle=\varTheta_{n}(w)\ \mathcal{A}_{n}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(w)\ ,

(5.12)

with the overall momentum-dependent phase factor

\displaystyle\varTheta_{n}(w)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,\sum_{i<j}\,p_{k_{i}}\cdot\theta\,p_{k_{j}}\bigg)\ .

(5.13)

Due to antisymmetry of the bivector
\theta
, the phase is cyclically invariant:
\varTheta_{n}(w)=\varTheta_{n}(w^{\circlearrowleft})
where
w^{\circlearrowleft}:=k_{n}k_{1}\cdots k_{n-1}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
, and symmetric under Kleiss–Kuijf reflection:

\varTheta_{n}(w)=\varTheta_{n}(\bar{w})^{-1}
where
\bar{w}=k_{n}k_{n-1}\cdots k_{1}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
. The ordering properties (
5.9
) then imply

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(w)=\mathcal{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(w^{\circlearrowleft})=(-1)^{n}\,\varTheta_{n}(\bar{w})^{-2}\ \mathcal{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(\bar{w})\ .

(5.14)

This argument will be corroborated later on when we embed the gauge theory as a low energy limit of open string theory with a constant background
B
-field.
It suggests that a twisted homotopy factorisation is possible for noncommutative Yang–Mills theory.

5.2
Strictification and twisted homotopy factorisation

Strictification in the second order formalism.

Just like ordinary Yang–Mills theory, because of the non-trivial
3
-bracket in
eq.

5.3c
, the
L_{\infty}
-structure of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory is
not
strict.
The twisted homotopy factorisation, and hence the homotopy double copy construction, only makes sense for strict
L_{\infty}
-algebras. This means finding a perturbatively equivalent theory with no vertices of order higher than three.

If one further wishes to double copy this theory with itself, a cubic Lagrangian whose Feynman diagrams produce tree-level gluon amplitudes in
twisted colour-kinematics dual form must be sought; in the commutative case this is always possible
[
80
]
.
This is done order by order in the multiplicity, by finding quasi-isomorphisms between the original theory and a strictified theory (organised by a strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra) that has manifest colour-kinematics duality. It extends the underlying cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{YM}_{\star})
to include auxiliary fields that enforce both the strict
L_{\infty}
-structure and the colour-kinematics duality, such that putting the auxiliary fields on-shell yields the higher order vertex.

We generalise the arguments of
[
12
]
to construct a strictification of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory that produces twisted colour-kinematics dual gluon amplitudes up to multiplicity four. For this, we introduce an auxiliary field
G\in\Omega^{1,2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
, where

\displaystyle\Omega^{1,2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}):=\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\otimes_{\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})}\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\ .

(5.15)

Written in component form, the cubic noncommutative Yang–Mills functional we consider is

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}^{\star}[A,G]&=\frac{1}{2}\,\int\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x\ \mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big(A_{\mu}\star\square\,A^{\mu}-2\,g\,A_{\nu}\star\partial_{\mu}[A^{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A^{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt+\sqrt{2}\,g\,\partial_{\bar{\alpha}}([A_{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)})\star G^{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}-G_{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}\star\square\,G^{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.16)

The resulting noncommutative gauge theory is perturbatively equivalent to the original theory after integration over the auxiliary field
G
, whose equation of motion is

\displaystyle\square\,G_{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}=\tfrac{1}{2\,\sqrt{2}}\,g\,\partial_{\bar{\alpha}}[A_{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ .

(5.17)

Substituting
eq.

5.17
into
eq.

5.16
gives back the noncommutative Yang–Mills functional (
5.6
), because of the
{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N))
-invariance of the cyclic structure.
This defines an
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
\psi^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}:\mathfrak{YM}_{\star}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 2}
whose non-vanishing components on gauge fields is given by

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(A)=A\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\psi_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(A_{1},A_{2})=\frac{g}{2\,\sqrt{2}\ \square}\ \mathrm{d}\otimes[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,

(5.18)

so that
G=\psi^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}_{2}(A,A)
.

The new cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 2}=\Big(\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 2})\,,\,\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}\,,\,\langle-,-\rangle_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}^{\star}\Big)\ .

(5.19)

In the following we focus only on its subspaces in degrees
1
and
2
for illustration, and drop the antighost as well as various auxiliary fields including the Nakanishi–Lautrup field. Its cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 2})
extends the four-term Yang–Mills cochain complex (
5.3a
) as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1,2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1,2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta\,\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\delta\,\mathrm{d}\,\otimes\,\mathbbm{1}}

(5.20)

with
2
-bracket modified to

\begin{split}&\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}\Big({\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}A_{1}\\
G_{1}\end{matrix}\bigg),\bigg(\begin{matrix}A_{2}\\
G_{2}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(A_{1},A_{2})+\sqrt{2}\,g\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big([A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(\delta\otimes\mathbbm{1})\,G_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+[A_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(\delta\otimes\mathbbm{1})\,G_{1}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\\[4.30554pt]
\sqrt{2}\,g\,\mathrm{d}\otimes[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\end{pmatrix}\ .\end{split}

(5.21)

The cyclic structure is extended by

\begin{split}\langle G,G^{+}\rangle_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}^{\star}=\int\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x\ \mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big(G_{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}\star G^{+\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(5.22)

for
G\in\Omega^{1,2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-1]
and
G^{+}\in\Omega^{1,2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]
.

We will first factorise the kinematic dependence from the differential form part, much like we did in
eq.

4.28
. For this, we introduce a graded vector space organising the differential forms for this theory through

{\small\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}\ :=\ \cdots\ \oplus\ \begin{matrix}\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})^{*}\\[3.87498pt]
\oplus\\[3.87498pt]
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})^{*}\otimes\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})^{*}\end{matrix}\ \oplus\ \begin{matrix}\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})^{*}[-1]\\[3.87498pt]
\oplus\\[3.87498pt]
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})^{*}\otimes\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})^{*}[-1]\end{matrix}\ \oplus\ \cdots\ .}\normalsize

(5.23)

We write
e^{\mu}
for the basis of covectors on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
in degree
0
and
e^{+\mu}
in degree
1
. We will also abbreviate
e^{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}:=e^{\bar{\alpha}}\otimes(e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu})
and
e^{+\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}:=e^{+\bar{\alpha}}\otimes(e^{+\mu}\wedge e^{+\nu})
, similarly to our conventions from
section

4.5
. This space carries the non-vanishing inner products

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle e^{\mu},e^{+\nu}\rangle\!\rangle:=\eta^{\mu\nu}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\langle\!\langle e^{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu},e^{+\bar{\beta}\rho\lambda}\rangle\!\rangle:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}\,(\eta^{\mu\rho}\,\eta^{\nu\lambda}-\eta^{\mu\lambda}\,\eta^{\nu\rho})\ .

(5.24)

Following
[
12
]
, we introduce a twist datum
\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}=\big(\tau_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}},\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}\big)
for
\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes L
, where
L
is the graded vector space (
3.33
). Its non-trivial actions on basis vectors in
\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}
are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tau_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(e^{\mu})&=e^{+\mu}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\quad,\quad\tau_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(e^{\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu})=-e^{+\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(e^{\mu},e^{\nu})&=3\,\big(e^{+\mu}\otimes(\partial^{\nu}\otimes\mathbbm{1}+\mathbbm{1}\otimes\partial^{\nu})-e^{+\nu}\otimes(\partial^{\mu}\otimes\mathbbm{1}+\mathbbm{1}\otimes\partial^{\mu})\big)\\
&\quad\,+\sqrt{2}\,{e}^{+\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}\otimes(\partial_{\bar{\alpha}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}+\mathbbm{1}\otimes\partial_{\bar{\alpha}})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(e^{\mu},e^{\bar{\alpha}\nu\rho})&=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\big(\eta^{\mu\nu}\,e^{+\rho}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\partial^{\bar{\alpha}}-\eta^{\mu\rho}\,e^{+\nu}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\partial^{\bar{\alpha}}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(e^{\bar{\alpha}\nu\rho},e^{\mu})&=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\big(\eta^{\mu\nu}\,e^{+\rho}\otimes\partial^{\bar{\alpha}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}-\eta^{\mu\rho}\,e^{+\nu}\otimes\partial^{\bar{\alpha}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.25)

Let
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}
be the cyclic strict
C_{\infty}
-algebra which was introduced in §
3.3
for the twisted homotopy factorisation of the star-commutator. It can be used to factorise the star-commutators of coordinate functions
A_{\mu},G_{\alpha\mu\nu}:\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{u}(N)
of the gauge fields and the auxiliary fields. In this way we obtain the twisted homotopy factorisation

\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 2}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}\otimes_{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}\ .

(5.26)

To see this, note that, after twisting against the
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}
, the first line of
eq.

5.25
easily reproduces the differential
\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}(A,G)=(\delta\,\mathrm{d}A,-(\delta\,\mathrm{d}\otimes\mathbbm{1})G)
of
eq.

5.20
on gauge fields and auxiliary fields. On gauge fields
A_{1},A_{2}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
, the third equality reproduces the non-strict noncommutative Yang–Mills
2
-bracket
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(A_{1},A_{2})
from
eq.

5.3b
, as well as the last term proportional to
\mathrm{d}\otimes[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}
of the strict
2
-bracket on degree
1
fields in
eq.

5.21
which lives in
\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\otimes_{\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})}\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\otimes\mathfrak{u}(N)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{2}^{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}}(e^{\mu}\otimes A_{1\mu}\,,\,e^{\nu}\otimes A_{2\nu})&=3\,g\,e^{+\mu}\otimes\big([\partial^{\nu}A_{1\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+[A_{1\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\partial^{\nu}A_{2\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\\
&\quad\,-3\,g\,e^{+\nu}\otimes\big([\partial^{\mu}A_{1\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+[A_{1\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\partial^{\mu}A_{2\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\\
&\quad\,+\sqrt{2}\,g\,e^{+\bar{\alpha}\mu\nu}\otimes\big([\partial_{\bar{\alpha}}A_{1\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+[A_{1\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\partial_{\bar{\alpha}}A_{2\nu}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.27)

Similarly, the last two equalities of the twist data from
eq.

5.25
reproduce the mixed terms among
A_{i}
and
G_{i}
in
eq.

5.21
. The cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}
is easily seen to be reproduced by
\langle\!\langle-,-\rangle\!\rangle\otimes\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}
.

Finally, the
C_{\infty}
-algebra part of the theory has already been factorised in
eq.

3.38
using the twist (
4.17
) applied to coordinate functions in
\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathfrak{u}(N))
. Hence we have constructed a twisted colour-kinematics dual factorisation

\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 2}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}\otimes_{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}}\big(\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(5.28)

This factorisation allows one to compute double copies up to multiplicity four scattering
[
12
]
with any theory, regardless of whether its strictification has manifest colour-kinematics duality.

Alternatively, by simply extracting the colour structure first analogously to
eq.

4.27
, we find that the factorisation (
5.28
) commutes with the twist data defined by
eq.

5.25
. Thus we can write the equivalent twisted homotopy factorisation

\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 2}=\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}\otimes_{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(5.29)

First order formalism.

In the foregoing discussion we set
d=4
, because a much more tractable way to strictify noncommutative Yang–Mills theory in four dimensions is to rewrite it in the first order formalism. Following
[
81
]
, this reformulates the theory as a non-topological deformation of noncommutative
BF
-theory with only cubic vertices; we will organise it below into a cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra akin to that of the noncommutative Chern–Simons theory of §
4.2
.
This strictification is
not
manifestly twisted colour-kinematics dual, and so it cannot be used to double copy itself. However, in this form the theory also admits a twisted homotopy factorisation, which can be used to double copy noncommutative Yang–Mills theory with any other theory that has manifest colour-kinematics duality and compute arbitrary
n
-point scattering amplitudes.

In four spacetime dimensions, the Hodge duality operator defines a complex structure on the vector space
\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
, i.e. an endomorphism
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\!:\!\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))\!\longrightarrow\!\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
, acting solely on the differential form part, such that
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}=-\mathbbm{1}
. The cubic noncommutative Yang–Mills functional for
an auxiliary
\mathfrak{u}(N)
-valued two-form field
B\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
and a gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is given by

S^{\star}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}[A,B]=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,B\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,B-B\wedge_{\star}F^{\star}_{A}\Big)\ ,

(5.30)

where as usual the corresponding noncommutative field strength is
F_{A}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}A+\frac{g}{2}\,[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}
in
\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
. Dropping the metric-dependent term defines noncommutative
BF
-theory, whose restriction to self-dual fields is the action functional of noncommutative self-dual Yang–Mills theory.

The equations of motion for
B
and
A
are found by using
\mathrm{ad}(\mathfrak{u}(N))
-invariance of the trace. They respectively read as

F_{A}^{\star}=\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,B\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\nabla_{A}^{\star}\,B=0\ ,

(5.31)

where
\nabla_{A}^{\star}:\Omega^{p}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
is the star-gauge covariant derivative (
4.9
).
Integrating out
B
by imposing the on-shell condition in
eq.

5.30
, we recover the noncommutative Yang–Mills functional (
5.1
).
For a gauge parameter
c\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
, the action functional (
5.30
) is invariant under the star-gauge transformations
\delta_{c}A=\mathrm{d}c+g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}
and
\delta_{c}B=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}B]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}
.

Noncommutative Yang–Mills theory in the first order formalism is organised by the cyclic
strict

L_{\infty}
-algebra

\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 1}=\Big(\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 1})\,,\,\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\,,\,\langle-,-\rangle_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}^{\star}\Big)\ ,

(5.32)

which is quasi-isomorphic to the cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
5.2
) through an injective
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\psi^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{2}}:\mathfrak{YM}_{\star}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 1}
.
The BV cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 1})
associated to this
L_{\infty}
-algebra is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{4}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))[-3]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}

(5.33)

which identifies the differential
\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
of degree
1
acting on homogeneous elements as

\begin{split}\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(c)=(\mathrm{d}c,0)\quad,\quad\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(A,B)=(\mathrm{d}A-\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,B,\mathrm{d}B)\quad,\quad\mu_{1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(B^{+},A^{+})=\mathrm{d}A^{+}\end{split}\ .

(5.34a)

The non-zero
2
-brackets
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
are given by

\begin{split}\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(c_{1},c_{2})=g\,[c_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\qquad&,\qquad\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big(c,{\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}A\\
B\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)=g\,\begin{pmatrix}[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\\[4.30554pt]
[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}B]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big(c,{\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}B^{+}\\
A^{+}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)=g\,\begin{pmatrix}[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}B^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\\[4.30554pt]
[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\end{pmatrix}\qquad&,\qquad\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(c,c^{+})=g\,[c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}c^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big({\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}A_{1}\\
B_{1}\end{matrix}\bigg),\bigg(\begin{matrix}A_{2}\\
B_{2}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)&=g\,\begin{pmatrix}[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\\[4.30554pt]
[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}B_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+[A_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}B_{1}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big({\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}A\\
B\end{matrix}\bigg),\bigg(\begin{matrix}B^{+}\\
A^{+}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)&=g\,\big([A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}A^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}+[B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}B^{+}]_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.34b)

Finally, as in §
4.2
, the cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle^{\star}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
pairs forms of complementary degrees:

\begin{split}\langle\alpha,\alpha^{+}\rangle_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}^{\star}:=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\alpha^{+})=\int\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{+})\ ,\end{split}

(5.34c)

for
\alpha\in\{c,A,B\}
.

Factorisation in the first order formalism.

We shall now demonstrate the homotopy factorisation of first order noncommutative Yang–Mills theory, which is similar to that of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory.
We first factorise the colour algebra using the twisting map from
section

4.3
, now applied to differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
.
It reproduces the star-commutator on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
as a twisted tensor product with
\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}_{2}
, and similarly the differential on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathfrak{u}(N))
as a twisted tensor product with
\tau_{1}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}
.

This yields the factorisation

\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 1}=\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}}\mathfrak{C}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\ ,

(5.35)

where
\mathfrak{C}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
is the colour-stripped
C_{\infty}
-algebra with underlying cochain complex

\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{C}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}})=\left(\hbox to345.12pt{\vbox to53.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 172.55556pt\lower-26.57056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{86.04166pt}{-15.55806pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\ .

(5.36)

Its brackets are given by colour-stripping the
L_{\infty}
-structure of
eq.

5.34
, so the differentials
m_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
are of the same form, while the
2
-brackets
m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
are simply given by exterior products as

\begin{split}m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(c_{1},c_{2})=g\,c_{1}\,c_{2}\ \ ,\ &\ m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big(c,{\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}A\\
B\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)=g\,\bigg(\begin{matrix}c\,A\\
c\,B\end{matrix}\bigg)\ \ ,\ \ m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big(c,{\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}B^{+}\\
A^{+}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)=g\,\bigg(\begin{matrix}c\,B^{+}\\
c\,A^{+}\end{matrix}\bigg)\\[4.0pt]
m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(c,c^{+})=g\,c\,c^{+}\quad,\ &\quad m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big({\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}A_{1}\\
B_{1}\end{matrix}\bigg),\bigg(\begin{matrix}A_{2}\\
B_{2}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)=g\,\bigg(\begin{matrix}A_{1}\wedge A_{2}\\
A_{1}\wedge B_{2}+A_{2}\wedge B_{1}\end{matrix}\bigg)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\Big({\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}A\\
B\end{matrix}\bigg),\bigg(\begin{matrix}B^{+}\\
A^{+}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\Big)&=g\,\big(A\wedge A^{+}+B\wedge B^{+}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.37)

We further factorise this
C_{\infty}
-algebra as a twisted tensor product

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\otimes_{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\ ,

(5.38)

where the kinematical vector space is identified as

\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\ :=\ \text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}[1]\quad\oplus\quad\begin{matrix}\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}\end{matrix}\quad\oplus\quad\begin{matrix}\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}[-1]\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}[-1]\end{matrix}\quad\oplus\quad\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{4}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}[-2]\ .

(5.39)

For a basis of covectors
e^{\mu}
on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
, we abbreviate
e^{\mu\nu\cdots}:=e^{\mu}\wedge e^{\nu}\wedge\cdots
; we distinguish the bases of
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}
in degrees
0
and
1
by denoting them respectively as
e^{\mu\nu}
and
e^{+\mu\nu}
. As in
section

4.3
, this vector space is equipped with a pairing given by wedging forms in complementary degrees and applying the Hodge duality operator
\mathrm{Tr}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}:\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{4}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{R}
to the resulting top form on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
, which on basis elements is given by

\begin{split}\mathrm{Tr}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}(e^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma})=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\ ,\end{split}

(5.40)

where
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
is the Levi–Civita symbol in four dimensions with
\epsilon^{0123}=1
.

The twist datum
\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}=\big(\tau_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}},\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\big)
is defined as follows. The non-zero values of the twisting map
\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}_{1}:\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\otimes\mathsf{End}(L)
for the graded vector space (
3.33
) are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}_{1}(1)=e^{\mu}\otimes\frac{\partial_{\mu}}{\square}&\quad,\quad\tau_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu})=e^{+\mu\nu}\otimes\frac{\partial_{\nu}}{\square}\quad,\quad\tau_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu\nu\rho})=e^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\otimes\frac{\partial_{\sigma}}{\square}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
&\tau_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu\nu})=-e^{+\rho\sigma}\otimes\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\mu\nu}\,\frac{1}{\square}+e^{\mu\nu\rho}\otimes\frac{\partial_{\rho}}{\square}\ .\end{split}

(5.41a)

This recovers the colour-stripped differential:
m_{1}^{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}}=m_{1}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
. For example, on degree
1
fields
(A,B)
the differential decomposes into

\displaystyle\begin{split}m_{1}^{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}}(e^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}+e^{\nu\rho}\otimes B_{\nu\rho})&=e^{+\mu\nu}\otimes\mu_{1}^{\rm Scal}\big(\tfrac{\partial_{\nu}}{\square}\,A_{\mu}\big)\\
&\quad\,-e^{+\rho\sigma}\otimes\mu_{1}^{\rm Scal}\big(\tfrac{1}{\square}\,\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}{}^{\mu\nu}\,B_{\mu\nu}\big)+e^{\nu\rho\sigma}\otimes\mu_{1}^{\rm Scal}\big(\tfrac{\partial_{\sigma}}{\square}\,B_{\nu\rho}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=e^{+\sigma\mu}\otimes(\partial_{\sigma}A_{\mu}-\epsilon_{\sigma\mu}{}^{\nu\rho}\,B_{\nu\rho})+e^{\mu\nu\rho}\otimes\partial_{\mu}B_{\nu\rho}\\[4.0pt]
&=(\mathrm{d}A-\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,B,\mathrm{d}B)\ .\end{split}

(5.41b)

The colour-stripped
2
-bracket
m_{2}^{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}}=m_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
is recovered using the following twisting map
\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}:\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\otimes\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\otimes\mathsf{End}(L)\otimes\mathsf{End}(L)
. Any bracket involving a degree
0
element is twisted in the same way, recovering the first four brackets of
eq.

5.37
from

\begin{split}\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(1,v)=v\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\ ,\end{split}

(5.41c)

for all
v\in\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}
.
Brackets between two degree
1
fields as well as brackets between a degree
1
field and a degree
2
field are recovered respectively by the non-vanishing values

\begin{split}\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu},e^{\nu})=e^{+\mu\nu}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\qquad,&\qquad\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu},e^{\nu\rho})=e^{\mu\nu\rho}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu\nu},e^{\rho})=-e^{\mu\nu\rho}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\qquad,&\qquad\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu},e^{\nu\rho\sigma})=e^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{\mu\nu},e^{+\rho\sigma})=e^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\qquad,&\qquad\tau_{2}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}(e^{+\mu\nu},e^{\rho\sigma})=-e^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\ .\end{split}

(5.41d)

For example,
2
-brackets between fields in degrees
1
and
2
are generated by the twisted bracket using the non-zero twist data from
eq.

5.41d
to reduce to top forms in
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{4}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}
:

\begin{split}&m_{2}^{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}}\big(e^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}+e^{\nu\rho}\otimes B_{\nu\rho}\,,\,e^{+\alpha\sigma}\otimes B^{+}_{\alpha\sigma}+e^{\beta\lambda\kappa}\otimes A^{+}_{\beta\lambda\kappa}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 142.26378pt=e^{\mu\beta\lambda\kappa}\,\otimes\mu_{2}^{\rm Scal}(A_{\mu},A^{+}_{\beta\lambda\kappa})+e^{\nu\rho\alpha\sigma}\,\otimes\mu_{2}^{\rm Scal}(B_{\nu\rho},B^{+}_{\alpha\sigma})\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 142.26378pt=g\,e^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\otimes\big(A_{\mu}\,A^{+}_{\nu\rho\sigma}+B_{\mu\nu}\,B_{\rho\sigma}^{+}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 142.26378pt=g\,\big(A\wedge A^{+}+B\wedge B^{+}\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.41e)

Finally, as in
section

4.3
the cyclic structure (
5.34c
) is reproduced by
\mathrm{Tr}_{\wedge^{\bullet}}\otimes\langle-,-\rangle_{\rm Scal}
, and altogether we have shown that the twisted homotopy factorisation of the cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra underlying noncommutative Yang–Mills theory in the first order formalism is given by

\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 1}=\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(N)}}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\otimes_{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(5.42)

As in
section

4.3
, both factorisations (
5.28
) and (
5.42
) identify the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory of §
3.7
as the zeroth copy of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory.

5.3
Noncommutative gauge theories on D-branes

We will now aim to understand the structural features of noncommutative Yang–Mills amplitudes that we have discussed as a natural consequence of the embedding of the noncommutative gauge theory into string theory as a low-energy limit of open strings in constant
B
-field backgrounds.

Open strings in Kalb–Ramond fields.

Consider the open string sigma-model with fields
X=(X^{\mu})
mapping from a Euclidean worldsheet
\Sigma
with boundary conditions corresponding to a stack of D
p
-branes filling flat space
\mathbbm{R}^{1,p}
in a closed string background with metric
g
, Kalb–Ramond two-form
b
and dilaton
\phi
, all of which are assumed to be constant. The D-brane worldvolume supports an abelian gauge field
A
of constant curvature
F
. This combines with the Kalb–Ramond field to the gauge-invariant Born–Infeld field strength
B:=b+F
, which can be regarded as an electromagnetic field on the D-branes that the open string endpoints are charged with respect to. We assume that
A
is a sum of gauge fields restricted to each connected component of the boundary
\partial\Sigma
, so that different branes can support independent
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge fluxes; this allows for open strings to end on D-branes with
different
overall background
B
-fields. For simplicity we suppose that the gauge flux
F
has the same rank
r\leq p+1
as
b
, and that
g_{\mu\nu}=0
whenever
\mu\in\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}
and
\nu\notin\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}
.

At tree-level in open string perturbation theory, the worldsheet
\Sigma
is a disk, or the conformally equivalent complex upper-half plane
\mathbbm{H}^{+}\subset\mathbbm{C}
. In the boundary conformal field theory on
\mathbbm{H}^{+}
, the presence of the
B
-field modifies the Neumann boundary conditions in the longitudinal directions to the D
p
-branes to the mixed boundary conditions

\displaystyle(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\bar{z}})\,g\,X+2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,(\partial_{z}+\partial_{\bar{z}})\,B\,X\,\big|_{\partial\,\mathbbm{H}^{+}}=0\ ,

(5.43)

where
{\rm Im}(z)\geq 0
and
\alpha^{\prime}
is the string Regge slope. The bulk propagator on the worldsheet
\mathbbm{H}^{+}
is then
[
82
]

\displaystyle\langle X^{\mu}(z,\overline{z})\,X^{\nu}(z^{\prime},\overline{z}^{\,\prime})\rangle_{\mathbbm{H}^{+}}=-2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,\big(g^{\mu\nu}\log|z-z^{\prime}|+D^{\mu\nu}\log|z-\overline{z}^{\,\prime}|+c^{\mu\nu}\big)\ ,

(5.44)

with the projector

\displaystyle D=-\frac{1}{g}+\frac{2}{g+2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,B}

(5.45)

and
c^{\mu\nu}
are arbitrary integration constants.

This Green’s function is defined on the double cover
\mathbbm{C}
of the upper-half plane
\mathbbm{H}^{+}
by worldsheet parity
\Omega:z\longmapsto\bar{z}
and is obtained using the method of images: starting from the free Green’s function
-2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,g^{\mu\nu}\log|z-z^{\prime}|^{2}
for the two-dimensional Laplace equation, one enforces the mixed boundary conditions by adding to it the contribution of an image charge symmetric with respect to reflection through the real line
z=\bar{z}
, which corresponds to worldsheet parity.
The propagator (
5.44
) is single-valued if the logarithmic branch cut is placed in the complex lower-half plane
\mathbbm{H}^{-}\subset\mathbbm{C}
.

The effective target space geometry seen by the open strings ending on the D
p
-branes consists of the open string metric
G
and the Poisson bivector
\theta
. They are related to the parameters
(g,b)
of the closed string background through the open-closed relations
[
69
]

\begin{split}G=g-(2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime})^{2}\,B\,\frac{1}{g}\,B\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\theta=-(2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime})^{2}\,\frac{1}{g+2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,B}\,B\,\frac{1}{g-2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,B}\ .\end{split}

(5.46)

This parametrization allows us to write the string propagator as

\begin{split}\langle X^{\mu}(z,\overline{z})\,X^{\nu}(z^{\prime},\overline{z}^{\,\prime})\rangle_{\mathbbm{H}^{+}}&=-2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,\bigg(g^{\mu\nu}\log|z-z^{\prime}|-g^{\mu\nu}\log|z-\overline{z}^{\,\prime}|+G^{\mu\nu}\log|z-\overline{z}^{\,\prime}|^{2}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}}{2\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}}\log\Big(\frac{z-\overline{z}^{\,\prime}}{\overline{z}-z^{\prime}}\Big)+c^{\mu\nu}\bigg)\ .\end{split}

(5.47)

On the boundary
\partial\,\mathbbm{H}^{+}=\mathbbm{R}
, the choice of constant counterterm
c^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}
reduces the propagator to the correlation function
[
69
]

\langle X^{\mu}(\tau)\,X^{\nu}(\tau^{\prime})\rangle_{\partial\mathbbm{H}^{+}}=-\alpha^{\prime}\,G^{\mu\nu}\log(\tau-\tau^{\prime})^{2}+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\ .

(5.48)

This implies that the operator product expansion of tachyon vertex operators in the limit
\tau\to\tau^{\prime}
with
\tau>\tau^{\prime}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p\cdot X}(\tau)\,\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,q\cdot X}(\tau^{\prime})=|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{\prime}\,p\cdot G\,q}\ \exp\big(\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,p\cdot\theta\,q\big)\ \mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,(p+q)\cdot X}(\tau^{\prime})+\cdots\ ,

(5.49)

where as usual the ellipses denote less singular terms as
\tau\to\tau^{\prime}
.
The term in the boundary propagator (
5.48
) involving
\theta
is a piecewise-constant function of
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
, so it does not contribute to correlation functions of
\tau
-derivatives of
X
.

Consider open strings with
\mathsf{U}(N)
Chan–Paton factors, and the tree-level scattering of
n
gluons of momenta
p_{i}
, polarizations
\zeta_{i}
, and Chan–Paton wavefunctions
\lambda_{i}=T^{a_{i}}
, with
i=1,\dots,n
.
The scattering amplitude for an ordering of colour factors
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
and insertion points
(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n})
on
\partial\,\mathbbm{H}^{+}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\rm open}(p,\zeta,a)_{G,\theta}&=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{u}(N)}(T^{a_{1}}\cdots T^{a_{n}})\\
&\qquad\times\int_{(\partial\,\mathbbm{H}^{+})^{\times n}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n})\ \Big\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\,\zeta_{i}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}X}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\ \mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{i}\cdot X}(\tau_{i})\Big\rangle_{G,\theta}\ ,\end{split}

(5.50)

where the subscripts
G,θ
indicate that we evaluate correlation functions and amplitudes as functions of the open string parameters
(G,\theta)
. The term multiplying the trace is the colour-stripped open string amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(1,\dots,n)_{G,\theta}
.
The vertex operators are inserted on
\partial\,\mathbbm{H}^{+}
in a definite cyclic order, and the measure
\mathrm{d}\mu
refers to the integral over the positions
\tau_{i}
modulo the action of
\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbbm{R})
by boundary conformal transformations.

The only
\theta
-dependence of the amplitude (
5.50
) is in the phase factor
\exp\big(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\sum_{i<j}\,p_{i}\cdot\theta\,p_{j}\big)
that comes from the expectation value of products of the tachyon vertex operators
\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{i}\cdot X(\tau_{i})}
in
eq.

5.49
.
This factor arises from the correlation function inside the
\mathrm{d}\mu
integral, and it is a piecewise-constant function of
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n}
that depends only on their cyclic ordering.
Since the cyclic ordering is kept fixed in evaluating the integral that gives the scattering amplitude, this factor multiplies an otherwise
\theta
-independent amplitude. Rescaling
\theta\to t\,\theta
for a parameter
t\in\mathbbm{R}
, the colour-stripped amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(1,\dots,n)_{G,t\,\theta}
thus factors as

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(1,\dots,n)_{G,t\,\theta}&=\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,t}{2}\,\sum_{i<j}\,p_{i}\cdot\theta\,p_{j}\Big)\ \mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(1,\dots,n)_{G,t=0}\end{split}\ .

(5.51)

In the limit
B\to 0
, the flat space string amplitudes are recovered.

We may interpret these equations in terms of an ordering of momenta
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
viewed as the word
w=1\cdots n\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
. The phase factor appearing in
eq.

5.51
may then be denoted as
\varTheta_{n}(w)
. This is the same as the phase (
5.13
) factoring tree-level amplitudes in noncommutative Yang–Mills theory.
The full amplitude is given by summing over the
(n-3)!
inequivalent orderings.
Since all the data of partial amplitudes respects an ordering
w\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
, all terms in
eq.

5.51
can be written in terms of
w
.

Seiberg–Witten limit.

To make the link with noncommutative gauge theories, we take the Seiberg–Witten limit
[
69
]
.
This scales the closed string metric to zero, while keeping both the open string metric
G
and Poisson bivector
\theta
fixed. It can be achieved by taking the usual low-energy limit
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
, but now correlated with the limit
g_{\mu\nu}\sim(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}\to 0
, or equivalently with the limit
B_{\mu\nu}\sim(\alpha^{\prime})^{-2}\to\infty
. In either of these scaling limits, the boundary propagator (
5.48
) becomes

\langle X^{\mu}(\tau)\,X^{\nu}(\tau^{\prime})\rangle^{\textrm{\tiny SW}}_{\partial\mathbbm{H}^{+}}=\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\ .

(5.52)

In the Seiberg–Witten limit, only the phase in the operator product expansion (
5.49
) remains as an overall pre-factor.
This has the effect of replacing ordinary multiplication of wavefunctions by the Moyal–Weyl star-product which quantizes the Poisson structure
\theta
.
The closed string propagator (
5.47
) diverges in this limit, as can also be seen at the level of the sigma-model action functional where the bulk kinetic term disappears and the worldsheet theory becomes a topological field theory on the boundary with a degenerate phase space.
BV quantisation of this topological string theory in the first order formalism reproduces star-products of fields in correlation functions of boundary observables
[
83
,
84
]
. The tree-level S-matrix is generated by the spacetime effective action functional of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,p}

[
69
]
, with the constant metric
G
replacing the
d=p+1
Minkowski metric
\eta
everywhere in §
5.1
.

Application of the Seiberg–Witten limit to
eq.

5.51
relates colour ordered tree-level scattering amplitudes in
\mathsf{U}(N)
noncommutative Yang–Mills theory with
N>1
to those of the corresponding commutative theory through the same phase factor (
5.13
):

\mathcal{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(w)_{G}=\varTheta_{n}(w)\ \mathcal{A}_{n}^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(w)_{G}\ ,

(5.53)

for
w\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
. Here the subscript
G
emphasises that all kinematic invariants used in the computation of amplitudes through this relation are evaluated with respect to the constant open string metric.

5.4
Bern–Carrasco–Johansson relations

The Bern–Carrasco–Johansson (BCJ) relations
[
5
]
are linear relations, whose coefficients are rational functions of Mandelstam variables, between tree-level partial amplitudes with different cyclic orderings that differ by the insertion position of a single gluon. They further reduce the number of independent subamplitudes at multiplicity
n
to
(n-3)!
.
After their advent it was realised that a natural explanation for them appears as the low-energy limit of monodromy relations in open string theory, see e.g.
[
85
]
.

BCJ relations for noncommutative Yang–Mills amplitudes are obtained in
[
79
]
, building on the BCFW recursion formulas discussed in
[
78
]
. This requires some care, as the noncommutative amplitudes contain essential singularities in the complex plane.
As we now explicitly demonstrate, these relations can also be derived from the same set of monodromy equations for open string scattering amplitudes in a constant Kalb–Ramond field that were found in
[
86
]
.

For an ordering of punctures
w=k_{1}\cdots k_{n}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
, the tachyonic part of the colour-stripped open string amplitude in
eq.

5.50
is given by an iterated integral of the corresponding Koba–Nielsen factor

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(w)_{G,t\,\theta}=\varTheta_{n}(w)\ \int_{\mathbbm{D}(w)}\ \prod_{i=1}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\tau_{k_{i}}\ \prod_{i<j}\,\big(\tau_{k_{i}}-\tau_{k_{j}}\big)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,p_{k_{i}}\cdot G\,p_{k_{j}}}\end{split}

(5.54)

over the domain
\mathbbm{D}(w)=\{(\tau_{k_{1}},\dots,\tau_{k_{n}})\in\mathbbm{R}^{n}\ |\ \tau_{k_{1}}<\cdots<\tau_{k_{n}}\}
.
For illustration we choose the trivial ordering
w=1\cdots n
.
We may then choose to single out
\tau_{1}
and integrate over it along the boundary of the worldsheet
\mathbbm{R}
.
The integrand is singular at
\tau_{1}=\tau_{i}
for
i>1
, where it has poles, while analytic everywhere else. Using this information, we analytically continue the integral over
\tau_{1}
to an integral over a suitable closed contour in the complex plane.

For each pole
\tau_{i}
, the integrand picks up a monodromy factor
\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\,\mathrm{i}\,\alpha^{\prime}\,p_{1}\cdot G\,\,p_{i}}
while the noncommutativity phase picks up a factor
\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,t\,p_{1}\cdot\theta\,p_{i}}
.
In addition, integration around each chamber
\tau_{i}<\tau_{1}<\tau_{i+1}
gives the partial amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(2,\dots.i,1,i{+}1,\dots,n)_{G,t\,\theta}
.
Introducing the massless Mandelstam invariants
s_{k_{i}k_{j}}:=2\,p_{k_{i}}\cdot G\,p_{k_{j}}
, and deforming the contour to infinity, it follows that the result of the contour integration yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\textrm{open}}(1,\dots,n)_{G,t\,\theta}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\varTheta_{n}(1\cdots n)\,\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\,\mathrm{i}\,\alpha^{\prime}\,(s_{12}+\cdots+s_{1i})}}{\varTheta_{n}(2\cdots i\,1\,i{+}1\cdots n)}\ \mathcal{A}_{n}^{\textrm{open}}(2,\dots,i,1,i{+}1,\dots,n)_{G,t\,\theta}=0\ .\end{split}

(5.55)

Now we take the Seiberg–Witten limit, in which the noncommutativity factor survives the low-energy limit
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
.
In this way, we obtain the Ward identities

\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\varTheta_{n}(n\cdots i{+}1\,1\,i\cdots 21)\ \mathcal{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(1,2,\dots,i,1,i{+}1,\cdots n)_{G}=0

(5.56)

as well as the BCJ relations

\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}\,\frac{s_{12}+\cdots+s_{1i}}{\varTheta_{n}(12\cdots i\,1\,i{+}1\cdots n)}\ \mathcal{A}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(1,2,\dots,i,1,i{+}1,\cdots n)_{G}=0

(5.57)

for noncommutative Yang–Mills theory.

5.5
Kawai–Lewellen–Tye relations

The factorisations discussed in
section

5.2
can be used to construct a variety of double copy theories from noncommutative Yang–Mills theory. As we saw already in
section

4.5
, these dual theories do not undergo any noncommutative deformation and coincide with their classical double copies. In particular, the homotopy double copy of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory with itself in the second order formalism yields ordinary perturbative gravity in
d
dimensions, or more exactly
\mathcal{N}=0
supergravity which involves a two-form and a dilaton in addition to the graviton
[
12
,
1
]
. Again we demonstrate that this is a natural consequence of properties of open string amplitudes in constant
B
-field backgrounds.

Kawai–Lewellen–Tye relations with
\boldsymbol{B}
-fields.

The Kawai–Lewellen–Tye (KLT) relations
[
4
]
are an explicit realisation of open-closed worldsheet duality for the tachyonic parts of string amplitudes.
A first topological argument comes from the observation that one can glue two oppositely oriented copies of a disk along their boundaries to form a Riemann sphere.
For open string amplitudes, punctures are inserted along the boundaries of the disks, so the gluing between amplitudes is a function only of kinematic invariants and the ordering of vertices along the two boundaries. The KLT relations have been understood more recently from several perspectives: through a more computationally efficient paradigm
[
26
,
87
]
, through an ultraviolet completion in
\alpha^{\prime}
of biadjoint scalar theory called Z-theory
[
88
]
, and through intersection theory on the moduli space of
n
-punctured Riemann spheres
[
26
,
89
]
. The one-loop extension of the tree-level KLT relations, involving torus and annulus amplitudes, is found in
[
90
]
.

Here we consider the tachyonic part
\mathcal{A}^{\rm open}_{n}(w)_{G}
of the open string amplitude for a given ordering of punctures (and associated momenta)
w\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
, and kinematical invariants calculated with respect to the open string metric
G
.
A closed form of the KLT relation is given in terms of a
momentum kernel

\boldsymbol{S}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}
linking the colour-stripped partial amplitudes to tree-level closed string amplitudes through

\mathscr{A}_{n,G}^{\rm closed}(p)=\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\right)^{n-3}\ \sum_{w,w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n-3}}\,\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(w)_{G}\ {\boldsymbol{S}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}\ \mathcal{A}^{\rm open}_{n}(w^{\prime})_{G}\ .

(5.58)

The reduced number of word letters here is due to the
\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbbm{C})
conformal invariance of the worldsheet theory, which enables one to fix three insertion points; following the standard choice we fix
z_{1}=0,

z_{n-1}=1
and
z_{n}=\infty
.
The
n
-point momentum kernel
\boldsymbol{S}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}
is not unique: it is derived from an iterated integral over
n-3
complex variables, so there are
n-3
equivalent contour integrals to choose from.

One possible choice is found in
[
91
]
. For
w=k_{1}\cdots k_{n}
and fixed ordering of the right insertion
w^{\prime}=1\cdots n
, define the operation

\mathtt{H}(k_{i},k_{j})_{w^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\ 1\quad\textrm{if the ordering of }k_{i},k_{j}\textrm{ is opposite in }w^{\prime}\ ,\\
\ 0\quad\textrm{otherwise}\ .\end{cases}

(5.59)

By conformal invariance, three points are fixed with labels
\{1,n-1,n\}
, so any summation over words in equations below is understood as leaving these letters fixed; for example, when writing
w\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
we mean
w=1\,w^{\circ}\,n{-}1\,n
for some
w^{\circ}\in\mathscr{W}_{n-3}
. We then set

\boldsymbol{S}_{n}(w|1\cdots n)_{G}=\left(\frac{2}{\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{n-3}\ \prod_{i=2}^{n-2}\,\sin\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,\Big(p_{1}\cdot G\,p_{k_{i}}+\textstyle{\sum\limits_{1\leq j<l\leq i}}\,\mathtt{H}(k_{j},k_{l})_{1\cdots n}\ p_{k_{j}}\cdot G\,p_{k_{l}}\Big)\ ,

(5.60)

and the full formula for the momentum kernel is simply obtained by permuting the right letters of
w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n-3}
in the algebra of ordered words on the letters
\{2,\ldots,n-2\}
. For example, unpacking
eq.

5.60
we find the momentum kernel

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}_{5}(12345|32145)_{G}=\big(\tfrac{2}{\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}}\big)^{2}\,\sin\big(\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,p_{1}\cdot G\,p_{2}\big)\sin\big(\pi\,\alpha^{\prime}\,p_{3}\cdot G\,(p_{1}+p_{2})\big)

(5.61)

involved in the gluing of multiplicity five string amplitudes.

The colour-stripped open string amplitudes appearing in the KLT formula (
5.58
) combine in an analytic way to compute closed string amplitudes. Using
eq.

5.51
they can be written in terms of open string amplitudes with a Kalb--Ramond background. This scales separately the left and right partial amplitudes with respective phase factors, and leaves the KLT formula invariant under redefinition of the momentum kernel according to
8
8

8

Recall from
section

5.3
that a situation with
\theta\neq\bar{\theta}
can arise if we allow for distinct worldvolume gauge fluxes on different stacks of D-branes, which give rise to distinct Born–Infeld field strengths
B\neq\bar{B}
in a fixed closed string background
(g,b)
. Even when
\theta=\bar{\theta}
, the notation aids in distinguishing the left-moving and right-moving open string sectors involved in the gluing of amplitudes.

\boldsymbol{S}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G,t\,\theta,\bar{t}\,\bar{\theta}}:=\varTheta_{n}(\bar{w})\ \boldsymbol{S}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}\ \bar{\varTheta}_{n}(\bar{w}^{\prime})\ .

(5.62)

We interpret this trivial modification as saying that, for a constant
B
-field, it is possible to glue open string amplitudes in a manner consistent with the fact that the Kalb–Ramond field is invisible to closed strings on a Riemann sphere. We thus propose the
B
-field modified KLT relations

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{n,G}^{\rm closed}(p)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\right)^{n-3}\ \sum_{w,w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n-3}}\,\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(w)_{G,t\,\theta}\ \boldsymbol{S}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G,t\,\theta,\bar{t}\,\bar{\theta}}\ \mathcal{A}_{n}^{\rm open}(w^{\prime})_{G,\bar{t}\,\bar{\theta}}\ ,

(5.63)

leaving unchanged the closed string scattering amplitudes.

Double copy relations.

In the
\alpha^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
limit, the closed string amplitudes taken with respect to the open string metric
G
reduce to pure gravity amplitudes
\mathscr{A}_{n,G}^{\textrm{\tiny GR}}(p)
on the background
\mathbbm{R}^{1,p}
with metric
G
.
Therefore the Seiberg–Witten limit of the KLT relations (
5.63
) gives the double copy relations

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{n,G}^{\textrm{\tiny GR}}(p)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\right)^{n-3}\ \sum_{w,w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n-3}}\,\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(w)_{G}\ \boldsymbol{S}^{\star\bar{\star}{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}\ \mathcal{A}_{n}^{\bar{\star}{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(w^{\prime})_{G}\ .

(5.64)

In terms of the massless Mandelstam invariants
s_{ij}=2\,p_{i}\cdot G\,p_{j}
computed with respect to the spacetime metric
G
, the new field theory KLT kernel is

\boldsymbol{S}^{\star\bar{\star}{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}=\varTheta_{n}(\bar{w})\ \bar{\varTheta}_{n}(\bar{w}^{\prime})\ \prod_{i=2}^{n-2}\,\Big(s_{1k_{i}}+\sum_{1\leq j<l\leq i}\,\mathtt{H}(k_{j},k_{l})_{w^{\prime}}\ s_{k_{j}k_{l}}\Big)\ ,

(5.65)

giving
\boldsymbol{S}^{\star\bar{\star}{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}=\varTheta_{n}(\bar{w})\,\boldsymbol{S}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}\,\bar{\varTheta}_{n}(\bar{w}^{\prime})
, the momentum kernel for gluing two copies of noncommutative gauge theories with respect to the original momentum kernel. This provides the explicit double copy construction of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory with itself to ordinary perturbative gravity: an ordinary graviton can also be regarded as composed of two
noncommutative
gluons.

A well-known feature of the field theory KLT kernel, originally derived in
[
10
]
, is that it can be expressed as the inverse matrix of double colour ordered biadjoint amplitudes
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}
that were discussed in
section

2.2
, here evaluated in the flat background metric
G
. This partial amplitude is the amplitude of a
\phi^{3}
-theory restricted to an ordering of external momenta, and it is related to the commutative momentum kernel as
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}=\boldsymbol{S}^{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}^{-1}
. In other words, tree amplitudes of the zeroth copy uniquely determine the double copy kernel. This connection has been more recently understood through different perspectives: the inverse of the string theory KLT kernel can be related to the doubly ordered tree amplitudes of the Z-theory prescription
[
87
, §2.2]
, while a simpler argument in the field theory limit is found in
[
92
]
.

In the noncommutative field theory, this relation applied to the modified momentum kernel (
5.65
) involves the sub-amplitudes

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\star\bar{\star}\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}=\varTheta_{n}(w)\ \mathcal{A}^{\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}\ \bar{\varTheta}_{n}(w^{\prime})

(5.66)

of the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory which we defined in
section

3.7
: the interaction vertex (
3.82
) keeps track of the two copies of noncommutative phase factors that are required by the noncommutative KLT kernel.
Thus the momentum kernel associated to the double copy of noncommutative gauge theory is sourced by the binoncommutative biadjoint scalar theory through

\boldsymbol{S}_{n}^{\star\bar{\star}{\textrm{\tiny YM}}}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}=\mathcal{A}^{\star\bar{\star}\textrm{\tiny BAS}}_{n}(w|w^{\prime})_{G}^{-1}\ .

(5.67)

Crucially, the noncommutative corrections preserve the rank of the matrix of double colour ordered amplitudes, which is
(n-3)!
, hence they satisfy the minimal rank condition and result in an admissible KLT kernel
[
27
]
.
This further vindicates our understanding that double copies of noncommutative gauge theories are the same as the double copies of the corresponding commutative gauge theories.

Remark 5.68
.

In the commutative case, the (non-strict) Yang–Mills
L_{\infty}
algebra
\mathfrak{YM}
can be viewed as a tensor product
\mathfrak{u}(N)\otimes\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{YM}}
of the gauge algebra with a kinematical
C_{\infty}
-algebra
[
93
]
. In a quasi-isomorphic description of
\mathfrak{YM}
inspired by open string field theory, this was used by
[
94
]
to show that a subspace of the
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{YM}}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\otimes\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{YM}}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}
, corresponding to states satisfying the level-matching
constraints of closed string theory, induces the cubic truncation of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra of double field theory. Following the prescription of the present paper, the same construction using our notion of twisted colour-stripping also gives ordinary double field theory as a doubling of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory, which results in the same
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{YM}}
.

5.6
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
Yang–Mills theory as a double copy

Most of what we have said so far in this section only applies to rank
N>1
, and it is natural to ask what is the fate of noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Yang–Mills theory from the double copy perspective. This is an interacting theory with non-trivial amplitudes that cannot be simply related to its commutative counterpart, which is the non-interacting Maxwell theory; scattering amplitudes in this theory are studied in e.g.
[
95
,
96
]
. Instead, we will interpret it analogously to what we did in
section

4.6
, by viewing
\mathfrak{u}(1)\simeq\mathbbm{R}=:\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}
as the kinematic vector space underlying the adjoint scalar theory introduced in
section

3.4
.

Consider the rank one limit of the factorisation of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory in the first order formalism from
eq.

5.42
, whose
L_{\infty}
-algebra is now elusively denoted as

\widehat{\mathfrak{YM}_{\star 1}(1)}=\mathfrak{Kin}_{\wedge^{0}}\otimes_{\tau^{\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)}}\big(\mathfrak{Kin}_{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}\otimes_{\tau^{{\textrm{\tiny YM}}_{1}}}\mathfrak{Scal}\big)\ .

(5.69)

The underlying cochain complex in this factorization is again given by
eq.

5.36
.
Comparing
eqs.

3.50
and
5.69
, and recalling the colour-kinematics duality of
section

3.5
, we conclude that noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Yang–Mills theory in the first order formalism is a double copy of the adjoint scalar theory of
section

3.4
with commutative Yang–Mills theory in the first order formalism, for any colour algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
