Symbolically, this double copy relation reads as

\displaystyle\text{$\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)$ Yang--Mills}\ =\ \text{Adjoint Scalar}\ \otimes\ \text{Ordinary Yang--Mills}\ .

(5.70)

This renders an interpretation of rank one noncommutative Yang–Mills theory as a gravitational theory, despite the absence of dynamical spin two fields, thus harvesting old anticipations discussed in
section

1
.

5.7
Noncommutative self-dual Yang–Mills theory

We can make the double copy relations of this section somewhat more precise in the self-dual sector of Yang–Mills theory in
d=4
dimensions, which we studied already in
section

3.8
. Noncommutative self-dual Yang–Mills theory was originally studied in
[
97
]
. In this sector the first order formalism of
section

5.2
is a
BF
-theory involving self-dual two-forms which describes (noncommutative) self-dual Yang–Mills theory. As shown by
[
66
]
, this theory (in the Leznov gauge) can be obtained from quantizing open
\mathcal{N}=2
strings in a constant background
B
-field in the Seiberg–Witten zero-slope scaling limit. For rank
N>1
, our twisted homotopy factorization is compatible with the twisted form of colour-kinematics duality that provides a double copy map of noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(N)
self-dual Yang–Mills theory with itself to
ordinary
self-dual gravity (perturbed around the open string metric
G
), along similar lines as discussed in
section

3.8
.

Restricting both sides of
eq.

5.70
to the self-dual sector in
d=4
dimensions provides a further corroboration of this double copy relation as a map to a gravitational theory. As we discussed in
section

3.8
, self-dual
\mathfrak{u}_{\star}(1)
Yang–Mills theory is the same theory as noncommutative self-dual gravity whose equation of motion is the deformed Plebański equation (
3.94
). There we also exhibited the explicit double copy construction of noncommutative self-dual gravity from the adjoint scalar theory with ordinary self-dual Yang–Mills theory. The connections of noncommutative self-dual gravity and Yang–Mills theory to Lorentz-invariant chiral higher-spin theories, discussed in
[
45
]
, hints at possible interpretations of the double copy relation (
5.70
) beyond the self-dual sector.

6
Final remarks

In this paper we have applied the homotopy algebraic formalism of
[
12
]
to the study of noncommutative gauge theories descending from the low-energy limit of open string theories with a stack of D-branes in constant
B
-fields.
We resolve the issue of colour and kinematic degrees of freedom mixing, which naively obstruct factorisation and colour-kinematics duality, by introducing a
twisted
form of factorisation and colour-kinematics duality.
We then construct the double copy of noncommutative gauge theories in this framework and show that it coincides with the commutative limit.
This apparently trivial result is substantiated by the well-known fact that noncommutativity is an open string effect which leads us to introducing a modified KLT relation; nevertheless the homotopy algebraic techniques used in this work are themselves interesting and may lead to further insights into the structure of noncommutative theories.
We also introduced a corresponding zeroth copy for noncommutative field theories, and checked that it plays the role of the inverse momentum kernel in our modified field theory KLT relation.

We illustrated our arguments by studying noncommutative deformations of Chern–Simons theory in three dimensions as well as of Yang–Mills theory in both first and second order formalisms.
We interpreted the special case of the rank one limits of noncommutative gauge theories as double copies themselves with the purely noncommutative adjoint scalar theory.
We also have reviewed applications of this formalism in the study of the self-dual sector of Yang–Mills theory and gravity by studying the semi-classical limits of a new binoncommutative scalar theory.
The diverse relations between theories are succinctly summarised in
fig.

1
.

Let us address here one final question: is there a way to modify our picture in such a way that noncommutative Yang–Mills theory can be double copied to a noncommutative theory of gravity? We assert that the answer is affirmative. As is well-known, noncommutative gravity involves a twisted form of diffeomorphism invariance, see e.g.
[
36
]
for a review. This twisted symmetry does not fit nicely into the standard
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism; see
[
98
]
for an explicit exposition. This in itself formally hints as to why the standard noncommutative gauge theories, which
are
organised systematically by
L_{\infty}
-algebras
[
18
,
19
]
, do not homotopy double copy to noncommutative gravity. However, we believe that this obstacle can be overcome by passing from
L_{\infty}
-algebras to
braided

L_{\infty}
-algebras, which organise noncommutative field theories with
braided
gauge symmetries
[
99
,
19
]
; this formalism was in fact initially developed with a homotopy algebraic approach to noncommutative gravity in mind.

By working with homotopy algebras in a symmetric monoidal category with non-trivial braiding, one can avoid the twisted factorizations and colour-kinematics duality that we had to introduce to stay in the usual categories of homotopy algebras, and instead work with the standard concepts, albeit in a braided setting. By a suitable extension of the homotopy double copy prescription to braided homotopy algebras, the double copy theory in this case would be encoded by a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra and so would have braided diffeomorphism symmetry. Hence it should describe noncommutative gravity. We plan to address this interesting perspective and its consequences in future work.

Appendix A
Homotopical techniques in quantum field theory

In this appendix we review the relevant aspects, used throughout the main text, of
L_{\infty}
-algebras and their uses in purely algebraic computations of correlation functions for perturbative quantum field theories.

A.1
Primer on quantum field theory

We begin by briefly recalling standard textbook material on the computation of correlation functions and scattering amplitudes in quantum field theory.
A free scalar field theory on Minkowski spacetime
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
is defined by the kernel of a hyperbolic operator
P
acting on the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
of smooth functions with rapidly decreasing derivatives of all orders at infinity.
The Minkowski–Fourier transform is an automorphism of this space.

For example, the massive Klein–Gordon operator
P=\square+m^{2}-\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon
is invertible on the Schwartz space with

\big(\square_{x}+m^{2}-\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon\big)\,G_{\mathrm{F}}(x,y)=\mathrm{i}\,\delta(x-y)\ .

(A.1)

The
\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon
-prescription is used to denote a choice of contour integral prescription which uniquely fixes the Feynman propagator
G_{\mathrm{F}}
.
It also moves states away from the mass-shell, dispelling the need to project zero-modes out, as the Feynman propagator is not defined on non-trivial fields in
\ker(P)
.
Thus

P\circ(-\mathrm{i}\,G_{\mathrm{F}})=(-\mathrm{i}\,G_{\mathrm{F}})\circ P=\mathbbm{1}

(A.2)

and we regard
-\mathrm{i}\,G_{\mathrm{F}}
as the partial inverse of
P
on the subspace of off-shell fields.

The representation of the Feynman propagator in momentum space as

G_{\mathrm{F}}(x,y)=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\int\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ \frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\,p\cdot(x-y)}}{p^{2}-m^{2}+\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon}

(A.3)

solves the Green’s identity (
A.1
) for all Schwartz functions and makes it a tempered distribution. We usually drop the explicit
\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon
-prescription to simplify notation.

Given an action functional
S[\phi]
for a field
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
, we construct correlation functions of the quantum field theory from the partition function for the theory coupled to an external source field
J\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
:

Z[J]=\int_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})}\,\mathscr{D}\phi\ \exp\Big(\,\mathrm{i}\,S[\phi]+\mathrm{i}\,\int\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x\ J\,\phi\Big)\ .

(A.4)

By separating the action functional into free and interacting parts as
S:=S_{\rm free}+S_{\rm int}
, the partition function has the functional derivative representation

Z[J]=\exp\Big(\,\mathrm{i}\,S_{\rm int}\Big[\frac{\delta}{\mathrm{i}\,\delta J}\Big]\Big)\,Z_{\rm free}[J]\ ,

(A.5)

where
Z_{\rm free}[J]
is the partition function for the free field theory coupled to
J
, which is evaluated by a functional Gaussian integration.

The off-shell
n
-point correlators
G_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, or Green’s functions, are defined by further functional differentiation with respect to
J\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
:

G_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\left.\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}^{n}}\,\frac{\delta^{n}}{\delta J(x_{1})\cdots\delta J(x_{n})}\,\frac{Z[J]}{Z[0]}\,\right|_{J=0}\ .

(A.6)

Both the partition function and the correlators have a Feynman diagram expansion in terms of unamputated graphs, which include propagators on external lines.

To find on-shell correlators, or scattering amplitudes, one appeals to the Lehman–Symanzik–Zimmermann (LSZ) reduction theorem.
This requires extending the field content to include states that are asymptotically on-shell. These are solutions of the free field equations of motion which are of Schwartz-type for any fixed time-slice of Minkowski space, the vector space of which we denote by
\ker_{\mathcal{S}}(P)
.
An early geometric treatment of this issue in the context of Chern–Simons perturbation theory was given in
[
74
]
, while a more modern treatment in terms of
L_{\infty}
-algebras is found in
[
100
,
101
]
.
We continue the Feynman propagator
{G}_{\mathrm{F}}
on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
trivially to
\widetilde{G}_{\mathrm{F}}
on the full field space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\oplus\ker_{\mathcal{S}}(P)
by setting

\widetilde{G}_{\mathrm{F}}\big|_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})}=G_{\mathrm{F}}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\ker(\widetilde{G}_{\mathrm{F}})=\ker_{\mathcal{S}}(P)\ .

(A.7)

Scattering amplitudes are obtained by applying the kinetic operator
P_{x}
at position
x\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
to a correlation function
G_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.
In a scalar field theory with on-shell normalised wavefunctions
\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{k}\cdot x_{k}}
, the
n
-point S-matrix element is given by

S_{n}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}):=\mathrm{i}^{n}\,\prod_{k=1}^{n}\ \int\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x_{k}\ \mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{k}\cdot x_{k}}\,P_{x_{k}}\,G_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\ .

(A.8)

This procedure amputates the external legs of a diagram with the correct pole structure and appends the correct wavefunctions, giving the S-matrix element as the multiple on-shell residue of the Fourier transformed Green’s function.
Only external states lying in
\ker_{\mathcal{S}}(P_{x_{k}})=\ker_{\mathcal{S}}(\widetilde{G}_{\mathrm{F}\,x_{k}})
contribute to the scattering amplitude.

For theories with fermions and/or gauge bosons, the in or out states to add depends on polarisation, spin and/or colour degrees of freedom.
The generalization of the LSZ formula for such quantum field theories is straightforward.

A.2
Homotopy algebra methods

We next review material on homotopy Lie (
L_{\infty}
-)algebras, and related homotopy commutative algebras, focusing on those features of direct relevance to the present paper. Unless otherwise explicitly stated, all vector spaces are defined over the field
\mathbbm{R}
of real numbers.

\boldsymbol{L_{\infty}}
-algebras.

An
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\})
consists of a
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded vector space
L=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,L^{k}
together with a (possibly infinite) sequence of brackets
\{\mu_{n}\}
which are multilinear maps of homogeneous degree
|\mu_{n}|=2-n
for
n\geq 1
:

\begin{split}\mu_{n}:L^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow L\ ,\quad v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}\longmapsto\mu_{n}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\ .\end{split}

(A.9)

They respect
strong graded antisymmetry
:

\mu_{n}(\ldots,v,v^{\prime},\ldots)=-(-1)^{|v|\,|v^{\prime}|}\,\mu_{n}(\ldots,v^{\prime},v,\ldots)\ ,

(A.10)

for homogeneous elements
v,v^{\prime}\in L
of degrees
|v|
and
|v^{\prime}|
, respectively.
In particular, on degree 1 elements
v,v^{\prime}\in L^{1}
, the 2-bracket is symmetric:
\mu_{2}(v,v^{\prime})=\mu_{2}(v^{\prime},v)
.

As opposed to graded antisymmetry, the Jacobi identity is only imposed in a weak sense.
The brackets fulfil (possibly infinitely many) higher Jacobi identities
\mathcal{J}_{n}=0
for
n\geq 1
, where
\mathcal{J}_{n}:L^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow L
is the map of degree
|\mathcal{J}_{n}|=3-n
defined by

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{n}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}):=\sum_{j+k=n}\,(-1)^{k}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(j;n)}\ &\chi(\sigma;|v_{1}|,\ldots,|v_{n}|)\\
&\quad\times\mu_{k+1}\big(\mu_{j}(v_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(j)}),v_{\sigma(j+1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(n)}\big)\ .\end{split}

(A.11)

The sum runs over permutations of degree
n
in the shuffle group
\mathrm{Sh}(j;n)\subset S_{n}
that represents how two decks of
j
and
n-j
cards get shuffled once together:

\mathrm{Sh}(j;n)=\big\{\sigma\in S_{n}\ \big|\ \sigma(1)<\cdots<\sigma(j)\ \textrm{ and }\ \sigma(j+1)<\cdots<\sigma(n)\big\}\ .

(A.12)

The Koszul sign factor
\chi
takes into account antisymmetry and grading degree, enabling one to express graded antisymmetry of higher brackets as

\mu_{n}(v_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(n)})=\chi(\sigma;|v_{1}|,\ldots,|v_{n}|)\ \mu_{n}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\ .

(A.13)

Let us look in detail at the identities
\mathcal{J}_{n}=0
for
n=1,2,3
. The first Jacobi identity imposes the requirement that the map
\mu_{1}
squares to zero, making
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{L}):=(L,\mu_{1})
into a cochain complex, whose cohomology is an
\mathbbm{R}
-module denoted by
{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})
. The second Jacobi identity is a compatibility condition which states that the differential
\mu_{1}
is a graded derivation of the 2-bracket
\mu_{2}
, i.e.
\mu_{2}:L\otimes L\longrightarrow L
is a cochain map of degree
0
. By precomposing with permutations
\sigma\in S_{3}
acting on vector labels
(1,2,3)
,
the third Jacobi identity is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{3}&=\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{3}-\mu_{2}\circ(\mu_{2}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\circ\big(\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 3}+{(123)}-{(23)}\big)\\
&\qquad\,+\mu_{3}\circ\big(\mu_{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 2}\big)\circ\big(\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes 3}+{(132)}-{(12)}\big)\ .\end{split}

(A.14)

This is the usual Jacobi identity for a graded Lie algebra up to terms involving
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{3}
, i.e. the Jacobi identity is controlled by a cochain map
\mu_{3}:L\otimes L\otimes L\longrightarrow L
of degree
-1
.
This weak but controlled definition is what is meant by a (strong) homotopy algebra:
The algebraic structure is respected up to a differential. In particular, the cohomology
{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})
with the induced maps is a graded Lie algebra.

Cyclic structures.

A
cyclic

L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra together with a non-degenerate bilinear form of degree
-3
:

\langle-,-\rangle:L\otimes L\longrightarrow\mathbbm{R}\ ,

(A.15)

which obeys the graded cyclic condition

\langle v_{1},\mu_{n}(v_{2},\ldots,v_{n+1})\rangle=(-1)^{n+n\,(|v_{1}|+|v_{n+1}|)+|v_{n+1}|\,(|v_{1}|+\cdots+|v_{n}|)}\ \langle v_{n+1},\mu_{n}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\rangle\ ,

(A.16)

for all
n\geq 1
and for homogeneous elements
v_{i}\in L
of degree
|v_{i}|
.

Maurer–Cartan theory.

A Maurer–Cartan element
A\in L^{1}
is an element of vanishing Maurer–Cartan curvature
f_{A}\in L^{2}
:

f_{A}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\ \mu_{n}(A^{\otimes n})=0\ .

(A.17)

This is called the Maurer–Cartan equation. It is identified as the equation of motion for gauge fields in a generalised gauge theory organised by a local
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\})
.

The Maurer–Cartan curvature
f_{A}\in L^{2}
satisfies the identity

\mathrm{d}_{A}f_{A}:=\mu_{1}(f_{A})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\ \mu_{n+1}(f_{A},A^{\otimes n})=0\ .

(A.18)

This is satisfied off-shell, i.e. when
f_{A}\neq 0
. It follows from a useful convergent series formula (see e.g.
[
75
, Appendix A]
) which enables one to recast it in terms of homotopy Jacobi identities (
A.11
) in
L^{3}
as

\mathrm{d}_{A}f_{A}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\ \mathcal{J}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})=0\ .

(A.19)

This is a generalisation of the Bianchi identity and is called the Maurer–Cartan–Bianchi identity. In a generalized gauge field theory it is identified as Noether’s second identity.

The Maurer–Cartan functional is an action functional for a Maurer–Cartan element
A\in L^{1}
on a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
. It is given by

S[A]:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\ \langle A,\mu_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle\ .

(A.20)

The choice of degree
-3
for the cyclic structure is chosen precisely so that
\langle A,\mu_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle
is valued in
\mathbbm{R}
(sitting in degree
0
) for gauge fields
A\in L^{1}
. Via the Leibniz rule for
\delta
together with cyclicity of the inner product, the variational principle
\delta S[A]=0
for arbitrary field variations
\delta A\in L^{1}
is equivalent to the Maurer–Cartan equation (
A.17
), since

\displaystyle\delta S[A]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\ \delta\langle A,\mu_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle=\langle\delta A,f_{A}\rangle\ .

(A.21)

Gauge variations are encoded in elements
c
of the vector space
L^{0}
.
The Maurer–Cartan action functional
S[A]
is invariant under the gauge transformation

\delta_{c}A=\mu_{1}(c)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\ \mu_{n+1}(A^{\otimes n},c)\ .

(A.22)

To see this, one uses cyclicity of the inner product to rewrite the gauge variation of
S[A]
for arbitrary gauge parameters
c\in L^{0}
in terms of the Noether identity (
A.18
):

\begin{split}\delta_{c}S[A]&=\langle\delta_{c}A,f_{A}\rangle=\langle c,\mathrm{d}_{A}f_{A}\rangle=0\ .\end{split}

(A.23)

Batalin–Vilkovisky theory.

We apply the Batalin–Vilkovisky (BV) formalism to a generalised gauge theory with local cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
.
For this, we consider superfields
{\boldsymbol{A}}\in\mathsf{Fun}(L[1])\otimes L
, where generally
L[p]
for
p\in\mathbbm{Z}
is the graded vector space obtained from
L
by shifting the degrees of its homogeneous subspaces by
-p
units:
L[p]^{k}:=L^{k+p}
. The brackets
\mu_{n}
and cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle
are extended in the obvious way to operations that make
\mathsf{Fun}(L[1])\otimes L
into a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra (see e.g.
[
75
]
); by an abuse of notation, we continue to denote these extended operations with the same symbols.
Applying homotopy Maurer–Cartan theory to this extended cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra, we retrieve the full BV action functional

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}[{\boldsymbol{A}}]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\ \langle{\boldsymbol{A}},\mu_{n}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{\otimes n})\rangle\ ,\end{split}

(A.24)

for
{\boldsymbol{A}}
a degree
1
superfield, also known as a contracted coordinate function.

A useful way of writing contracted coordinate functions on the BV field space is as follows. Let
\{\mathtt{e}^{I}\}\subset L
be a basis for the local cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}
, where
I
is a DeWitt multi-index representing colour and kinematic degrees of freedom. Then degree
1
superfields may be decomposed as
{\boldsymbol{A}}=\mathtt{e}^{I}\otimes\mathtt{A}_{I}
, where
\{\mathtt{A}_{I}\}
includes the BRST ghosts
c\in L^{0}
and the gauge fields
A\in L^{1}
, as well as their antifields
c^{+}\in L^{3}
and
A^{+}\in L^{2}
, together with ghosts-for-ghosts in negative degrees and corresponding antifields in
L^{k}
for
k\geq 4
.

In the quantum field theory, correlation functions are determined from the gauge-fixed partition function sourced by a degree
2
external superfield
{\boldsymbol{J}}\in\mathsf{Fun}(L[1])\otimes L
:

Z[{\boldsymbol{J}}]=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\,\mathscr{D}\mathtt{A}_{\mathcal{L}}\ \exp\big(\,\mathrm{i}\,S_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}[{\boldsymbol{A}}]+\mathrm{i}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{J}},{\boldsymbol{A}}\rangle\big)\big|_{\mathcal{L}}\ ,

(A.25)

where
\mathcal{L}
is a Lagrangian submanifold of the BV field space
L
with respect to the natural symplectic structure induced by the cyclic inner product, which is parametrized by fields
\mathtt{A}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Analogously to
eq.

A.5
, the Feynman expansion for the partition function of a Maurer–Cartan theory in the superfield formalism is given by

\begin{split}Z[{\boldsymbol{J}}]=\exp\bigg(\,\mathrm{i}\ {\textstyle{\sum\limits_{n=2}^{\infty}}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\ \Big\langle\frac{\delta}{\mathrm{i}\,\delta\mathtt{J}},\mu_{n}\Big(\big(\frac{\delta}{\mathrm{i}\,\delta\mathtt{J}}\big)^{\otimes n}\Big)\Big\rangle}\bigg)\,\int_{\mathcal{L}}\,\mathscr{D}\mathtt{A}_{\mathcal{L}}\ \exp\big(\,\mathrm{i}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{A}},\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}})\rangle+\mathrm{i}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{J}},{\boldsymbol{A}}\rangle\big)\big|_{\mathcal{L}}\ .\end{split}

(A.26)

By decomposing
{\boldsymbol{J}}=\mathtt{e}_{I}\otimes\mathtt{J}^{I}
, where
\{\mathtt{e}_{I}\}\subset L^{*}\simeq L[3]
is a dual basis to
\{\mathtt{e}_{I}\}
with respect to the cyclic structure, the gauge-fixed
n
-point correlator is then

G_{n}[\mathtt{A}_{I_{1}},\dots,\mathtt{A}_{I_{n}}]\big|_{\mathcal{L}}=\left.\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}^{n}}\,\frac{\delta^{n}}{\delta\mathtt{J}^{I_{1}}\cdots\delta\mathtt{J}^{I_{n}}}\,\frac{Z[{\boldsymbol{J}}]}{Z[{\boldsymbol{0}}]}\,\right|_{{\boldsymbol{J}}={\boldsymbol{0}}}\ .

(A.27)

Physically meaningful information is extracted from this correlator upon contracting the fields, which is done using the cyclic structure of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Morphisms of
\boldsymbol{L_{\infty}}
-algebras.

Let us now describe the category of (cyclic)
L_{\infty}
-algebras. Let
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\})
and
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}=(L^{\prime},\{\mu_{n}^{\prime}\})
be
L_{\infty}
-algebras. An
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\psi:\mathfrak{L}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
is a sequence
\{\psi_{n}\}
of multilinear graded antisymmetric maps
\psi_{n}:L^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow L^{\prime}
of degree
|\psi_{n}|=1-n
for
n\geq 1
such that all maps are compatible with all brackets.

Informally, for each fixed partition
n=j+k
and
n=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l}
of
n
tensor powers of the graded vector space
L
, one can regard compatibility in terms of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\otimes k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime\,\otimes l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{j}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\psi_{k_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{l}^{\prime}}

(A.28)

The sum of all possible combinations of diagrams commute and give the formula

\begin{split}&\sum_{j+k=n}\,(-1)^{k}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(j;n)}\,\chi(\sigma;|v_{1}|,\ldots,|v_{n}|)\ \psi_{k+1}\big(\mu_{j}(v_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(j)}),v_{\sigma(j+1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(n)}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\quad=\sum_{l=1}^{n}\,\frac{1}{l!}\ \sum_{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l}=n}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{l-1};n)}\,\chi(\sigma;|v_{1}|,\dots,|v_{n}|)\ (-1)^{\zeta_{k_{1},\dots,k_{l}}(\sigma;|v_{1}|,\dots,|v_{n}|)}\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\times\mu^{\prime}_{l}\big(\psi_{k_{1}}(v_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(k_{1})}),\ldots,\psi_{k_{l}}(v_{\sigma(n-k_{l}+1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(n)})\big)\ ,\end{split}

(A.29)

for all
n\geq 1
, where

\displaystyle\zeta_{k_{1},\dots,k_{l}}(\sigma;|v_{1}|,\dots,|v_{n}|)=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq l}\,k_{i}\,k_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\,(l-i)\,k_{i}+\sum_{i=2}^{l}\,(1-k_{i})\ \sum_{k=1}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l-1}}\,|v_{\sigma(k)}|\ .

(A.30)

Note that the left-hand side of
eq.

A.29
is formally equivalent to the higher Jacobi identity (
A.11
) if one replaces
\psi_{k+1}
with
\mu_{k+1}
.

A
strict

L_{\infty}
-algebra is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra of the form
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
, i.e. such that
\mu_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 3
. A strict
L_{\infty}
-algebra is the same thing as a differential graded (dg-)Lie algebra, but regarded as an object in the category of
L_{\infty}
-algebras. This perspective allows for more flexibility and stronger properties, as morphisms between dg-Lie algebras are more restrictive than
L_{\infty}
-morphisms.

If
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
are moreover endowed with cyclic structures
\langle-,-\rangle_{L}
and
\langle-,-\rangle_{L^{\prime}}
, respectively, then an
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\psi:\mathfrak{L}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
extends to a morphism of cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras if

\langle\psi_{1}(v_{1})\,,\,\psi_{1}(v_{2})\rangle_{L^{\prime}}=\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{L}

(A.31)

and

\sum_{j+k=n}\,\langle\psi_{j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j})\,,\,\psi_{k}(v_{j+1},\ldots,v_{n})\rangle_{L^{\prime}}=0\ ,

(A.32)

for
n\geq 3
. For strict
L_{\infty}
-algebras, a cyclic inner product is the same thing as a non-degenerate invariant bilinear form on a dg-Lie algebra.

Recall that to any
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\})
there is an associated cochain complex given by
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{L})=(L,\mu_{1})
with cohomology
{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})
.
An
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism

\psi:\mathfrak{L}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
is an
L_{\infty}
-morphism whose induced map on cohomology
\psi_{*}:{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})\longrightarrow{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism. In contrast to dg-Lie algebras, quasi-isomorphism is an equivalence relation on the category of
L_{\infty}
-algebras.

Under an
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\psi:\mathfrak{L}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
, the Maurer–Cartan theory for
\mathfrak{L}
is mapped to the Maurer–Cartan theory for
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
according to

\begin{split}A\longmapsto A^{\prime}&=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\ \psi_{n}(A^{\otimes n})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\ \mu^{\prime}_{n}(A^{\prime\,\otimes n})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
f_{A}\longmapsto f_{A^{\prime}}&=\psi_{1}(f_{A})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\ \psi_{n+1}(f_{A},A^{\otimes n})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
c\longmapsto c^{\prime}&=\psi_{1}(c)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\ \psi_{n+1}(A^{\otimes n},c)\ .\end{split}

(A.33)

Thus Maurer–Cartan elements
A\in L^{1}
are sent to Maurer–Cartan elements
A^{\prime}\in L^{\prime\,1}
. In particular, an
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
\psi
induces an isomorphism between the moduli spaces of Maurer–Cartan elements in
L
and
L^{\prime}
modulo gauge transformations. In the language of field theory, this means that quasi-isomorphic theories are classically equivalent.

Minimal models and recursion relations.

Any
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\})
has an associated
minimal model

\mathfrak{L}^{\circ}=({\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L}),\{\mu_{n}^{\circ}\})
, which is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra based on the graded cohomology
{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})
with respect to the differential
\mu_{1}
. To close the
L_{\infty}
-structure of the minimal model, infinitely-many higher brackets need to be introduced using
homotopy transfer
of
L_{\infty}
-algebras, which achieves the process of integrating out fields in the path integral. These have the interpretation of effective currents generating scattering amplitudes in quantum field theory, as we will discuss later on.

As originally shown in
[
102
,
103
]
, any
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}
is isomorphic to a direct sum of its minimal model and an
L_{\infty}
-algebra with trivial cohomology as well as trivial higher brackets. The
L_{\infty}
-algebras
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{L}^{\circ}
are homotopy equivalent due to the existence of a deformation retract given by a map
{\sf h}:L\longrightarrow L
of degree
-1
, called a contracting homotopy, and a choice of section
\mathsf{e}:{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow L
of degree
0
which fit into the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf h}}

(A.34)

for the natural projection
{\sf p}:L\,-\!\!\!\twoheadrightarrow{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})
of degree
0
, and yield the Hodge–Kodaira decomposition

\mathbbm{1}_{L}=\mathsf{e}\circ{\sf p}+{\sf h}\circ\mu_{1}+\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h}\ .

(A.35)

Thus the condition that the degree 0 maps
{\sf h}\circ\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h}
are projectors is relaxed in general.

One can construct a recursion formula
[
101
]
for the quasi-isomorphism
\psi_{n}:{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow L
by considering Maurer–Cartan elements
v_{1}^{\circ},\dots,v_{n}^{\circ}\in{\sf H}^{1}(\mathfrak{L})
.
The original proof of quasi-isomorphism between homotopy algebras and their minimal models, given in
[
103
]
for the case of homotopy associative (
A_{\infty}
-)algebras, does not require the triple of maps
({\sf p},{\sf h}\circ\mu_{1},\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h})
to be projections.
However, given the Hodge–Kodaira decomposition (
A.35
), one can redefine the contracting homotopy
{\sf h}
such that
{\sf h}\circ{\sf h}=0
, and
\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h}
as well as
{\sf h}\circ\mu_{1}
do become projectors. Thus
eq.

A.35
is replaced by

\begin{split}\mathbbm{1}_{L}&=\mathsf{e}\circ\sf p+{\sf h}\circ\mu_{1}+\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h}\ ,\quad{\sf h}={\sf h}\circ\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h}\circ\mu_{1}\ .\end{split}

(A.36)

The recursion relations are given by

\begin{split}\psi_{1}(v^{\circ}_{1})&=\mathsf{e}(v_{1}^{\circ})\end{split}

(A.37a)

and

\begin{split}&\psi_{n}(v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n})\\[4.0pt]
&\quad=-\sum_{j=2}^{n}\,\frac{1}{j!}\ \sum_{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{j}=n}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1};n)}\,\chi(\sigma;|v_{i}^{\circ}|,\dots,|v_{n}^{\circ}|)\ (-1)^{\zeta_{k_{1},\dots,k_{j}}(\sigma;|v_{1}^{\circ}|,\dots,|v_{n}^{\circ}|)}\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times({\sf h}\circ\mu_{j})\big(\psi_{k_{1}}(v_{\sigma(1)}^{\circ},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{\sigma(k_{1})}),\ldots,\psi_{k_{j}}(v^{\circ}_{\sigma(i-k_{j}+1)},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{\sigma(i)})\big)\ .\end{split}

(A.37b)

Similarly, the brackets on the minimal model can be recursively computed as

\begin{split}\mu_{1}^{\circ}(v_{1}^{\circ})&=0\end{split}

(A.37c)

and

\begin{split}&\mu_{n}^{\circ}(v_{1}^{\circ},\ldots,v_{n}^{\circ})\\[4.0pt]
&\quad=\sum_{j=2}^{n}\,\frac{1}{j!}\ \sum_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{j}=n}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{j-1};n)}\,\chi(\sigma;|v_{i}^{\circ}|,\dots,|v_{n}^{\circ}|)\ (-1)^{\zeta_{k_{1},\dots,k_{j}}(\sigma;|v_{1}^{\circ}|,\dots,|v_{n}^{\circ}|)}\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times({\sf p}\circ\mu_{j})\big(\psi_{k_{1}}(v_{\sigma(1)}^{\circ},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{\sigma(k_{1})}),\ldots,\psi_{k_{j}}(v^{\circ}_{\sigma(i-k_{j}+1)},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{\sigma(i)})\big)\ .\end{split}

(A.37d)

In field theory, the differential
\mu_{1}:L^{1}\longrightarrow L^{2}
generates the free field equations; its inverse is the Feynman propagator on the subspace of field space that excludes
\ker(\mu_{1})
.
The contracting homotopy
{\sf h}:L^{2}\longrightarrow L^{1}
then has the interpretation of the Feynman propagator extended trivially to on-shell states, as we have seen in §
A.1
, with gauge fixing imposed by setting
{\sf h}(v)=0
for
v\in L^{1}
.
If the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}
carries a cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle
, then its minimal model
\mathfrak{L}^{\circ}
also has a cyclic structure given by pullback
\langle v_{1}^{\circ},v_{2}^{\circ}\rangle_{\circ}:=\langle\mathsf{e}(v^{\circ}_{1}),\mathsf{e}(v^{\circ}_{2})\rangle
.
It should be stressed that quasi-isomorphic theories do not necessarily yield the same quantum theory even though they are equivalent at the classical level.
We will see an explicit example of this later on, in which we compute tree-level scattering amplitudes using the minimal model.

Twisted tensor products and
\boldsymbol{C_{\infty}}
-algebras.

Let
\mathfrak{R}=(R,m_{1},m_{2})
be a differential graded algebra with underlying graded vector space
R=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,R^{k}
, or equivalently a strict homotopy algebra.
The
twisted tensor product
of the underlying cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{R})=(R,m_{1})
with a graded vector space
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,V^{k}
is the cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(V\otimes_{\tau}\mathfrak{R})=(V\otimes R,m_{1}^{\tau})
, with underlying graded vector space
V\otimes R=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,(V\otimes R)^{k}
whose homogeneous subspaces are
(V\otimes R)^{k}=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}\,V^{i}\otimes R^{j}
.
Using the standard Sweedler notation, the twist map

\begin{split}\tau_{1}:V\longrightarrow V\otimes\mathsf{End}(R)\quad,\quad v\longmapsto\tau_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}(v)\otimes\tau_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}(v)\end{split}

(A.38a)

defines a
twisted differential
m_{1}^{\tau}:V\otimes R\longrightarrow V\otimes R
which is the linear degree 1 map squaring to zero given by

m_{1}^{\tau}(v\otimes r)=(-1)^{|\tau_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}(v)|}\ \tau^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}_{1}(v)\otimes m_{1}\big(\tau^{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}_{1}(v)(r)\big)\ ,

(A.38b)

for
v\in V
and
r\in R
.

One can extend this construction to the full dg-algebra
\mathfrak{R}

[
12
]
.
Introducing a second twist map

\begin{split}\tau_{2}:V\otimes V&\longrightarrow V\otimes\mathsf{End}(R)\otimes\mathsf{End}(R)\ ,\\
v_{1}\otimes v_{2}&\longmapsto\tau^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}_{2}(v_{1},v_{2})\otimes\tau_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}(v_{1},v_{2})\otimes\tau_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny(3)}}(v_{1},v_{2})\ ,\end{split}

(A.38c)

we define a
twisted
2
-bracket

m_{2}^{\tau}:(V\otimes R)^{\otimes 2}\longrightarrow V\otimes R
which is the bilinear degree 0 map given by

\begin{split}m_{2}^{\tau}(v_{1}\otimes r_{1},v_{2}\otimes r_{2})&=(-1)^{|v_{2}|\,|r_{1}|}\ \tau^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}_{2}(v_{1},v_{2})\otimes m_{2}\big(\tau_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}(v_{1},v_{2})(r_{1})\,,\,\tau_{2}^{\textrm{\tiny(3)}}(v_{1},v_{2})(r_{2})\big)\ ,\end{split}

(A.38d)

for
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
.
Depending on the homotopical structure of
\mathfrak{R}
, an appropriate choice of twist datum
\tau:=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
turns
V\otimes_{\tau}\mathfrak{R}:=(V\otimes R,m_{1}^{\tau},m_{2}^{\tau})
into a differential graded algebra.

This often involves the notion of a
C_{\infty}
-algebra
: a
C_{\infty}
-algebra is a commutative algebra in the category of
A_{\infty}
-algebras. In the main text we only use
strict

C_{\infty}
-algebras (often representing a kinematic algebra), which are equivalently described as differential graded commutative algebras. Concretely, a strict
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{C}=(C,m_{1},m_{2})
is a cochain complex
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathfrak{C})=(C,m_{1})
with a cochain map
m_{2}:C\otimes C\longrightarrow C
of degree
0
which is graded commutative and associative:

\displaystyle m_{2}(c_{1},c_{2})=(-1)^{|c_{1}|\,|c_{2}|}\,m_{2}(c_{2},c_{1})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad m_{2}\big(m_{2}(c_{1},c_{2}),c_{3}\big)=m_{2}\big(c_{1},m_{2}(c_{2},c_{3})\big)\ ,

(A.38e)

for
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in C
.

The twisted tensor product is a way to factorize strict homotopy algebras in a unique fashion, as necessitated by the double copy; one needs to check by hand that it defines a dg-algebra. In this paper we start from a known homotopy algebra, and factorise it in terms of a twist datum
\tau
such that the given homotopical structure is recovered.
In our examples, the twisted tensor product mixes the types of homotopy algebras: the twisted tensor product of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{R}
yields a
C_{\infty}
-algebra, while conversely the twisted tensor product of a
C_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{R}
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

A.3
Scattering in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

Tree-level amplitudes.

To connect the formalisms of §
A.1
and §
A.2
, we start from the observation of
[
100
,
101
]
that scattering amplitudes in a quantum field theory computed with the LSZ reduction formula (
A.8
) can be interpreted in terms of the cyclic structure on the minimal model for the local
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
which organises the field theory.
Indeed, for the scattering of fields which are sections of a (trivial) vector bundle
E
over Minkowski spacetime
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
, one considers the field content in
L^{1}={\Gamma}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},E)\oplus{\sf H}^{1}(\mathfrak{L})
, that is, interacting fields with the correct asymptotic behaviour and on-shell states at infinity.

For the minimal model of the BV theory, we trade the brackets
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\geq 2}
on
\mathsf{Fun}(L[1])\otimes L
for infinitely-many brackets
\{\mu_{n}^{\circ}\}_{n\geq 2}
on
\mathsf{Fun}\big({\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})[1]\big)\otimes{\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L})
.
Since
\mu_{1}^{\circ}=0
, the corresponding gauge-fixed partition function (
A.26
) is explicitly calculated through the Legendre transformation

Z^{\circ}[{\boldsymbol{J}}]=\exp\big(\,\mathrm{i}\,S^{\circ}[{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}]+\mathrm{i}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{J}},{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}\rangle_{\circ}\big)\big|_{{\sf p}(\mathcal{L})}\ ,

(A.39)

where
{\boldsymbol{J}}=-f_{{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}}
and

\displaystyle S^{\circ}[{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}]:=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\ \langle{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ},\mu_{n}^{\circ}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ\,\otimes n})\rangle_{\circ}\ .

(A.40)

One recognises the argument of the exponential as the generating functional of connected tree diagrams, which is given in terms of the Maurer–Cartan functional
S^{\circ}[{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}]
of the minimal model.

Using
eq.

A.27
for the minimal model partition function, it follows that the
n
-point correlation functions are given entirely in terms of the algebraic structure of the minimal model. That is, the Maurer–Cartan elements of the minimal model
{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}
generate partial tree-level amplitudes

\mathcal{M}_{n}(1,\ldots,n)=\langle{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}_{1},\mu_{n-1}^{\circ}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{2}^{\circ},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}_{n})\rangle_{\circ}\big|_{{\sf p}(\mathcal{L})}\ ,

(A.41)

with the full amplitude given by taking the sum over all planar ordered permutations of external particles.
In summary, the computation of tree-level amplitudes is reduced to the problem of solving the recursion relations in
eq.

A.37b
. That the recursion relations of the minimal model yield tree-level amplitudes was already realised in early work on the subject
[
103
]
.

Perturbiner expansions.

The link between perturbiner expansions and
L_{\infty}
-recursion relations was established by
[
40
]
.
Working at the level of vector spaces, a
perturbiner element
of a field theory with
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}=(L,\{\mu_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
is a Maurer–Cartan element
A^{\circ}\in{\sf H}^{1}(\mathfrak{L})
of the minimal model
\mathfrak{L}^{\circ}=({\sf H}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{L}),\{\mu_{n}^{\circ}\},\langle-,-\rangle_{\circ})
.
Since the quasi-isomorphisms from
eq.

A.37b
map Maurer–Cartan elements to Maurer–Cartan elements, from a perturbiner element
A^{\circ}
one reconstructs an on-shell interacting field of the full theory as

A=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{n!}\ \psi_{n}(A^{\circ\,\otimes n})\ \in\ L^{1}\ .

(A.42)

The on-shell free fields
A^{\circ}
in the minimal model source the interactions. Hence
eq.

A.42
has the interpretation of an expansion in terms of plane waves, first introduced in
[
104
]
.
Indeed, the Berends–Giele relations provide an alternative way to compute the quasi-isomorphism and thus the tree-level scattering amplitudes. This is discussed further below.

The perturbiner expansion can also be extended to compute both off-shell tree and loop correlation functions, see e.g.
[
105
,
106
]
, as we tacitly do in several parts of the main text. Loop-level scattering amplitudes can be computed recursively from minimal models of quantum
A_{\infty}
-algebras using homological perturbation theory
[
107
]
, generalising the tree-level Berend–Gieles recursion relations;
for correlation functions of scalar field theories this corresponds to taking the trivial projection
{\sf p}=0
in
eq.

A.36

[
108
]
.

Word combinatorics.

In explicit examples, one is faced with combinatorics on words, which we briefly overview here.
This arises because there is a homomorphism between the shuffle groups in the standard
L_{\infty}
-relations and tensor products of word algebras.

Let
M\subset\mathbbm{N}
be a subset of positive integers of cardinality
n
.
An
ordering

w
of
M
is a word of length
|w|=n
with no repeated letters; equivalently, a word is an ordering if it is maximal in the set
M
.
Denote by
\mathscr{O}_{M}
the set of orderings of
M
, and by
\mathscr{W}_{M}
the
\mathbbm{R}
-vector space generated by orderings, with the inner product

(w_{1},w_{2})=\delta_{w_{1},w_{2}}\ ,

(A.43)

that is, two words
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are orthogonal if they are distinct.
Let
\mathscr{L}_{M}
be the set of multilinear Lie polynomials in
\mathscr{W}_{M}
, i.e. elements of the free Lie algebra
\mathscr{F}(M)
on the set
M
of maximal length:

\mathscr{L}_{M}=\mathscr{F}(M)\cap\mathscr{W}_{M}\ ,

(A.44)

in the free associative algebra on
M
.
For example, if
M=\{1,2,3,4\}
, then
[[[1,2],4],3]\in\mathscr{L}_{M}
, whereas
[1,2]\notin\mathscr{L}_{M}
.

As we often work on the set
M=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, in the following we will replace the subscripts
M
with
n
on all sets defined above.
For example,
\mathscr{L}_{n}
is the set of multilinear Lie polynomials of length
n
.
Given a word
w=k_{1}\cdots k_{n}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
, we denote its transpose by
\bar{w}=k_{n}\cdots k_{1}
, with
|w|=|\bar{w}|=n
.

The left bracketing operation on words of length
n
is the map

\begin{split}\ell:\mathscr{W}_{n}&\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}\ ,\\
k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}\cdots k_{n}&\longmapsto[[\cdots[[k_{1},k_{2}],k_{3}],\ldots],k_{n}]=(-1)^{n-1}\ \mathrm{ad}_{k_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathrm{ad}_{k_{3}}\circ\mathrm{ad}_{k_{2}}(k_{1})\ .\end{split}

(A.45)

For example,
\ell(123)=[[1,2],3]=123-213-312+321\in\mathscr{L}_{3}
.
9
9

9

One can similarly define a right bracketing map
r(k_{1}\cdots k_{n-1}k_{n})=\mathrm{ad}_{k_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathrm{ad}_{k_{n-1}}(k_{n})=(-1)^{n-1}\ \ell(\overline{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}})
,
but it will not be used here.

For non-empty words
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
, one has

\begin{split}\ell\big(w\,\ell(w^{\prime})\big)=[\ell(w),\ell(w^{\prime})]\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell^{2}(w)&=|w|\,\ell(w)\ .\end{split}

(A.46)

Conversely, by the Dynkin–Specht–Wever lemma, if a word
w
satisfies
\ell(w)=|w|\,w
then
w
is a Lie polynomial.
Any Lie polynomial
\varGamma\in\mathscr{L}_{n}
can be expanded as

\varGamma=\sum_{w\in\mathscr{L}_{n-1}}\,(1w,\varGamma)\ \ell(1w)\ ,

(A.47)

as the bases
1w
and
\ell(1w)
for
\mathscr{L}_{n}^{*}
and
\mathscr{L}_{n}
respectively with
w\in\mathscr{O}_{n-1}
are dual:

(1w,\ell(1w^{\prime}))=\delta_{ww^{\prime}}\ .

(A.48)

The shuffle product
\shuffle
on words
w
and
w^{\prime}
of lengths
n
and
n^{\prime}
, respectively, is defined inductively from the empty word
\varnothing
:

w\shuffle\varnothing=\varnothing\shuffle w=w\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad w\,a\shuffle w^{\prime}\,a^{\prime}=(w\shuffle w^{\prime}\,a^{\prime})\,a+(w\,a\shuffle w^{\prime})\,a^{\prime}\ ,

(A.49)

for non-empty letters
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{N}
.
Shuffled words are orthogonal to Lie polynomials, so

\ell(w\shuffle w^{\prime})=0\ .

(A.50)

Following
[
46
]
, we define the binary tree map
b:\mathscr{W}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}
recursively by

\begin{split}b(a)=a\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad b(w)=\frac{1}{s_{w}}\,\sum_{w=w_{1}\sqcup w_{2}}\,[b(w_{1}),b(w_{2})]\ ,\end{split}

(A.51)

where
a\in\mathbb{N}
and
s_{w}
is the kinematical invariant Mandelstam variable associated with momenta labelled by the letters in
w
. The sum runs over deconcatenations of the word
w\in\mathscr{W}_{n}
into two non-empty words
w_{1}\in\mathscr{W}_{i}
and
w_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{n-i}
.

To make contact with the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism, we consider the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}
between the shuffle group (
A.12
) and word algebras. We view elements
\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(i;n)
as words of length
n
with image
\mathrm{im}(\sigma)=(w_{1},w_{2})
; notice that there exists a conjugate element
\bar{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Sh}(n-i;n)
with image
\mathrm{im}(\bar{\sigma})=(w_{2},w_{1})
. This defines the map

\begin{split}\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{Sh}(i;n)\longrightarrow\mathscr{W}_{i}\otimes\mathscr{W}_{n-i}\quad,\quad\sigma\longmapsto\mathrm{im}(\sigma)=w_{1}\otimes w_{2}\ ,\end{split}

(A.52)

where here we consider the action of
\sigma
on
M=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Biadjoint scalar theory.

As an explicit example illustrating these techniques, we consider the commutative biadjoint scalar theory from
section

2.2
.
Because of the homotopy factorisation (
2.21
), we can use the colour-stripped version of Berends–Giele recursion
[
104
,
41
]
.
The idea is to consider a superposition of
n
on-shell fields
\phi^{\circ}\in{\sf H}^{1}(\mathfrak{BAS})=\ker(\square)
and expand the interacting field in a basis of the tensor product. We label colour by the index sets
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
and
\{\bar{a}_{1},\ldots,\bar{a}_{n}\}
for the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
, respectively, and associated momenta by
\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\right\}
. We take our expansion to be valued in the free Lie algebra
\mathscr{F}(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}})

[
109
]
; this requires an extension of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{BAS}
to values in
\mathscr{F}(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}})
, which however we do not indicate explicitly in the notation. For a word
w=k_{1}\cdots k_{n}
on
n
letters, we denote
T^{w}:=T^{a_{k_{1}}}\cdots T^{a_{k_{n}}}
, and similarly for
\bar{T}^{w}
, as well as
p_{w}:=p_{k_{1}}+\cdots+p_{k_{n}}
.

The biadjoint field
\phi\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathscr{F}(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}}))
admits an expansion in terms of Lie words

\phi(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\ \sum_{w,w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}}\,\phi_{w|w^{\prime}}^{\circ}\ \mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{w}\cdot x}\ T^{w}\otimes\bar{T}^{w^{\prime}}\ ,

(A.53)

where
\mathscr{W}_{n}
is the algebra of words of length
n
, and
\phi_{w|w^{\prime}}^{\circ}
is a function valued in double words modulo shuffles:

\phi^{\circ}_{w\shuffle\tilde{w}|w^{\prime}}=\phi^{\circ}_{w|\tilde{w}\shuffle w^{\prime}}\ .

(A.54)

We call
\phi_{w|w^{\prime}}\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\mathscr{F}(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}})^{*})
the
Berends–Giele double current
.
The currents are computed recursively starting from the one-particle states
\phi^{\circ}_{1|1}\in{\sf H}^{1}(\mathfrak{BAS})
satisfying the Maurer–Cartan equation of the minimal model
\mathfrak{BAS}^{\circ}
.
The first few terms can be found from the equation of motion
\square\,\phi=\frac{g}{2}\,\llbracket\phi,\phi\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}
, giving

\phi^{\circ}_{12|12}=\phi^{\circ}_{21|{21}}=-\phi^{\circ}_{12|{21}}=-\phi^{\circ}_{21|{12}}=\frac{g}{s_{12}}\ .

(A.55)

The Berends–Giele expansion must match with the
L_{\infty}
-recursion relations.
Hence we identify
eq.

A.53
with
eq.

A.42
, where on the left-hand side we use the single-particle expansion
\phi^{\circ}(x)=\sum_{w,w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}}\,\phi^{\circ}_{w|w^{\prime}}\ \mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{w}\cdot x}\ T^{w}\otimes\bar{T}^{w^{\prime}}
. In this way we arrive at

\psi_{n}(\phi^{\circ\,\otimes n})=n!\,\sum_{w,w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}}\,\phi_{w|w^{\prime}}^{\circ}\ \mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{w}\cdot x}\ T^{w}\otimes\bar{T}^{w^{\prime}}\ .

(A.56)

This is checked by using
eq.

2.15
in
eq.

A.37b
for the contracting homotopy
{\sf h}
given by the Feynman propagator acting as

\displaystyle G_{\rm F}(\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p\cdot x})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{p^{2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p\cdot x}\ ,

(A.57)

so that the recursion relations are

\psi_{n}(\phi^{\circ\,\otimes n})=\frac{\mathrm{i}\,g}{2}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\bigg(\begin{matrix}n\\
i\end{matrix}\bigg)\ G_{\rm F}\big(\llbracket\psi_{i}(\phi^{\circ\,\otimes i}),\psi_{n-i}(\phi^{\circ\,\otimes n-i})\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}\big)\ .

(A.58)

Direct computation of the
L_{\infty}
-recursion matches with the Berends–Giele expansion found in
eq.

A.55
up to second order, when inserted back in
eq.

A.53
to reproduce the factorised bracket:

\begin{split}\psi_{1}(\phi^{\circ})&=\mathsf{e}(\phi^{\circ})=\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{1}\cdot x}\ T^{a_{1}}\otimes\bar{T}^{\bar{a}_{1}}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\psi_{2}(\phi^{\circ},\phi^{\circ})&=\mathrm{i}\,g\,G_{\rm F}\big(\llbracket\phi^{\circ},\phi^{\circ}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}\big)=\frac{g}{s_{12}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{12}\cdot x}\ [T^{a_{1}},T^{a_{2}}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes[\bar{T}^{\bar{a}_{1}},\bar{T}^{\bar{a}_{2}}]_{\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}\ .\end{split}

(A.59)

The tree-level scattering amplitudes (
A.41
) between identical particles are given by the cyclic structure and the higher brackets of the minimal model

\displaystyle\mathfrak{BAS}^{\circ}=\Big(\ker(\square)[-1]\xrightarrow{\ 0\ }\mathrm{coker}(\square)[-2]\ ,\ \big\{\mu_{n}^{\circ\textrm{\tiny BAS}}\big\}\ ,\ \langle-,-\rangle_{\circ\textrm{\tiny BAS}}\Big)\ .

(A.60)

For
{\sf p}
the trivial projection onto on-shell states, the
L_{\infty}
-recursion in
eq.

A.37d
is given by

\mu^{\circ}_{n}(\phi^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,\phi^{\circ}_{n})=-\frac{g}{2}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(i;n)}\,{\sf p}\circ\llbracket\psi_{i}(\phi^{\circ}_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,\phi^{\circ}_{\sigma(i)})\,,\,\psi_{n-i}(\phi^{\circ}_{\sigma(i+1)},\ldots,\phi^{\circ}_{\sigma(n)})\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}\ .

(A.61)

This has the first non-trivial bracket

\displaystyle\mu_{2}^{\circ}(\phi^{\circ}_{1},\phi^{\circ}_{2})=-g\,\llbracket\phi^{\circ}_{1},\phi^{\circ}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}\ \mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,p_{12}\cdot x}\ .

(A.62)

Using the cyclic structure (
2.16
), the partial three-point amplitude is then given by

\mathcal{M}_{3}^{\textrm{\tiny BAS}}(1,2,3|1,2,3)=-g\,f^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}}\,\bar{f}^{\bar{a}_{1}\bar{a}_{2}\bar{a}_{3}}\ (2\pi)^{d}\,\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})\ ,

(A.63)

as expected.
